Use A+ and A− at the side to change the text size. Listen 1x 0:00 / 0:00 A two-month bird flu outbreak at a Utah mink farm has raised concerns about worker safety, H5N1 surveillance and gaps in the government response. Explore Interactive Learning: Where Can Bird Flu Exposure Be Interrupted? A prolonged outbreak of H5N1 bird flu at a mink farm in Utah has raised concerns among infectious disease experts about gaps in animal disease control, farmworker monitoring and coordination between state and federal health agencies. The outbreak reportedly continued for more than two months at a farm raising thousands of mink, prompting questions about whether additional measures were needed to prevent the virus from spreading (1✔). A ProPublica investigation published on October 23, 2026, reported that officials became aware of infected mink in August but did not order the animals to be culled or actively monitor exposed workers for symptoms. Despite these concerns, Utah health authorities reported no confirmed human bird flu infections in the state as of October 23, and the risk to the general public remains low (2✔). Bird Flu Bird flu (avian influenza/avian flu) is a disease caused by an influenza virus (H5N1) that primarily affects birds but can infect humans also. H5N1 Bird Flu Spreads Through Utah Mink Farm The outbreak occurred at C&T Fenn Farms, a fur farm west of Provo, approximately an hour from Salt Lake City. According to ProPublica, Utah agriculture officials learned in mid-August that mink were dying from highly pathogenic avian influenza A(H5N1) (1✔). The farm houses thousands of mink raised for their fur. Although bird flu is best known for affecting wild birds and poultry, the virus has also infected mammals, including dairy cattle, cats and mink. The investigation reported that seven captive mink had tested positive according to federal animal surveillance data. However, this figure should not be confused with the total number of animals infected or killed during the outbreak, which was not independently established in the published report. The concern is that continued transmission within a dense population of susceptible mammals may provide additional opportunities for the influenza virus to change. Federal laboratory analysis reportedly linked the virus found in the infected mink to the H5N1 strain circulating among US dairy cattle. The exact route by which the virus entered the mink farm remains uncertain. Australia's H5N1 Bird Flu Cases Cross 200 As Wildlife Faces Growing Threat Australia has recorded 220 H5 bird flu detections in wild birds, raising concerns about native wildlife while authorities monitor the virus and maintain a low human health risk. Why Mink Could Become an Important Host for Bird Flu Mink are particularly relevant to influenza research because they are susceptible to respiratory viruses that can infect multiple animal species. Scientists have identified influenza receptors in mink respiratory tissues that make these animals useful for studying how viruses adapt to different hosts. When an influenza virus repeatedly infects mammals, researchers watch for genetic changes that could alter its ability to infect cells, reproduce or spread between hosts. Another concern is viral reassortment. Influenza viruses have segmented genomes, and when two compatible influenza viruses infect the same cell, they can exchange genetic segments, potentially producing a virus with new characteristics. Neither mutation nor reassortment automatically creates a virus capable of sustained human-to-human transmission. However, prolonged outbreaks can increase opportunities for changes that scientists need to investigate. The initial genetic sequences from the Utah mink outbreak reportedly did not reveal particularly concerning mutations. Experts have nevertheless called for continued sequencing of newer samples to determine whether the virus has changed during ongoing transmission (1✔). ONE HEALTH • BIRD FLU EXPLORER Where Can Bird Flu Exposure Be Interrupted? Bird flu can spread among susceptible animals and sometimes infect people. Explore four safeguards that help reduce exposure and detect infections. 1. Farm Biosecurity 2. Worker Protection 3. Health Monitoring 4. Testing and Response Farm Biosecurity Animal surveillance, movement restrictions and safe handling procedures can help identify infections and reduce opportunities for further animal exposure. Worker Protection Appropriate respirators, eye protection, gloves and protective clothing can reduce exposure to infected animals and contaminated materials. Health Monitoring The CDC recommends monitoring potentially exposed people for symptoms during exposure and for 10 days after the last known exposure. Testing and Response Eye redness, fever, cough or other compatible symptoms following exposure should prompt rapid medical assessment and appropriate bird flu testing. Important: This is a simplified prevention illustration. It does not show a confirmed transmission event or imply that any safeguard guarantees complete protection. Utah reported no confirmed human bird flu cases as of October 23, 2026. Source: CDC and Utah Department of Health and Human Services. Why Australia's First H5N1 Bird Flu Case Is a Global Concern Australia confirms its first mainland H5N1 bird flu case after the virus was found in seabirds, raising concerns for wildlife and biosecurity. Did Utah's Bird Flu Response Leave Workers Exposed? A major concern raised by the investigation involved the handling of the outbreak by animal-health and public-health authorities. Utah state veterinarian Amanda Price reportedly sought assistance from the US Department of Agriculture (USDA). Federal officials indicated that they did not have a dedicated regulatory program for mink comparable to those used for certain other livestock, or an approved bird flu vaccine specifically for mink. The response differed from standard control measures used in commercial poultry outbreaks, where infected flocks may be depopulated to reduce transmission. Several infectious disease experts interviewed by ProPublica questioned why the Utah farm's mink were not culled. However, decisions about animal removal also involve animal welfare, legal authority, disease-control strategy and economic considerations. The central public-health concern was the absence of active health monitoring among workers potentially exposed to infected animals. According to the investigation, Utah officials had previously modified their approach to worker monitoring following concerns that proactive health checks discouraged cooperation from farms. The CDC recommends that people exposed to infected birds or other animals be monitored for signs of respiratory illness and conjunctivitis beginning after their first exposure and continuing for 10 days after their last exposure (3✔). Nepal Closes Central Zoo After Bird Flu Found in Birds and Mammals Nepal shuts its Central Zoo indefinitely after H5N1 bird flu is detected in birds and mammals, raising concerns over animal and public health. Red Eyes Can Be an Early Warning Sign of H5N1 Infection One detail in the investigation involved a mink farmworker who reportedly had a visibly red eye. The farm owner told ProPublica that the redness resulted from dust exposure and that the local health department had not recommended testing. Eye redness alone does not establish H5N1 infection. Conjunctivitis can result from allergies, irritation and many common infections. However, the CDC identifies eye redness or irritation as an important possible symptom of bird flu in people with recent exposure to infected animals (4✔). Other symptoms may include fever, cough, sore throat, runny nose, muscle aches, fatigue and headache. More severe illness can involve breathing difficulties, pneumonia and other complications. Importantly, fever is not always present. A person exposed to infected animals may require evaluation even when eye irritation is the only reported symptom. The CDC recommends prompt testing of symptomatic people with relevant exposures. Depending on the clinical presentation, laboratory testing may involve respiratory specimens, eye swabs or both (5✔). How Can Bird Flu Spread from Infected Animals to Humans? Avian influenza A(H5N1) viruses usually circulate among birds, but certain strains have shown the ability to infect mammals. Human infections can occur when virus-containing material from infected animals reaches the eyes, nose or mouth, or when infectious droplets or particles are inhaled. Potential exposures include handling sick or dead animals, working in contaminated barns, touching infected animal secretions or contacting contaminated equipment without appropriate protection. Farmworkers, veterinarians, animal handlers and others who work closely with infected animals may face greater occupational risk than the general public. There is currently no evidence that the Utah mink outbreak has resulted in sustained human-to-human transmission. The World Health Organization's September 2026 assessment also found no reports of sustained person-to-person transmission associated with the influenza events it reviewed (6✔). This distinction is essential. Animal outbreaks can be serious public-health concerns without meaning that a human pandemic has begun. Utah's Earlier Mink Outbreak Offers a Warning The latest incident has drawn comparisons with coronavirus outbreaks on Utah mink farms during the COVID-19 pandemic. During 2020, large numbers of farmed mink became infected with SARS-CoV-2, the virus responsible for COVID-19. Previous investigations documented extensive animal illness and raised concerns about transmission between humans and mink. Scientists studying these outbreaks highlighted the importance of surveillance at the human-animal interface, where infections can pass between species. The comparison does not mean H5N1 will follow the same transmission pattern as SARS-CoV-2. The viruses are biologically different and have distinct characteristics. However, the earlier experience illustrates why coordinated animal testing, worker health monitoring and transparent reporting can be important when an infection spreads through farmed mammals. What Can Protect Farmworkers From Bird Flu? The CDC recommends a combination of workplace controls and personal protective equipment for people working around potentially infected animals (7✔). Depending on exposure level and job duties, protective measures may include appropriate respirators, eye protection, gloves, protective clothing and procedures for safely removing contaminated equipment. Workers should avoid unprotected contact with sick or dead animals and with potentially contaminated secretions, bedding and surfaces. Anyone who develops compatible symptoms after animal exposure should contact a healthcare provider or local health department promptly and report the exposure history. The CDC recommends antiviral treatment, typically with oseltamivir, as soon as possible for patients with suspected bird flu following a relevant exposure. Treatment should not be delayed while waiting for laboratory confirmation when clinical suspicion is sufficiently high (8✔). Antiviral preventive treatment may also be considered for certain high-risk exposures following a clinician's assessment. For the general public, avoiding contact with sick or dead wildlife and following official food-safety and animal-health guidance remain sensible precautions. Could the Utah Mink Outbreak Trigger a Human Pandemic? The possibility of a future influenza pandemic is one reason scientists monitor H5N1 infections in mammals so closely. A virus would need to acquire and maintain characteristics that allow efficient transmission between people before it could drive sustained human spread. Experts are concerned that continued transmission among susceptible animals could create opportunities for such adaptations. Nevertheless, the possibility of viral evolution should not be confused with evidence that a pandemic-capable virus has already emerged. The available information does not establish that the Utah outbreak has infected humans or produced a strain capable of sustained transmission between people. Utah's public-health website reported zero confirmed human bird flu infections as of October 23, 2026, while continuing to advise people with animal exposure to seek medical assessment if symptoms develop (2✔). The most immediate priorities are appropriate animal disease control, timely laboratory testing, genomic surveillance and protection of workers with direct exposure. Bird Flu Surveillance Must Connect Animal and Human Health The Utah mink farm outbreak has renewed discussion about the One Health approach, which recognizes that human, animal and environmental health are interconnected. Effective surveillance involves communication between veterinarians, agricultural officials, healthcare providers, laboratories and public-health authorities. The reporting by ProPublica raises questions about whether gaps in these systems delayed appropriate assessment of occupational risks. The investigation documents serious concerns, but the extent to which any particular response decision affected disease transmission remains uncertain. For now, the Utah outbreak is a warning about preparedness rather than evidence of an emerging human bird flu pandemic. The lesson is that protecting people from animal-borne infections begins with detecting outbreaks early, monitoring those most exposed and responding before isolated animal infections become harder to contain. Bird Flu Slams Sac County Turkey Flock, Reynolds Declares Iowa Disaster
新闻简报On October 28, 2026, Iowa confirmed its eighth HPAI outbreak of the year in a commercial turkey flock in Sac County, marking the first such case since December 2025. The flock, estimated at 44,000 to 44,400 birds, triggered an immediate disaster proclamation by Governor Kim Reynolds, effective through November 27. This follows a broader national bird flu outbreak since February 2022, with Iowa reporting 30.8 million birds lost to the virus alone. The CDC maintains that the risk to the general public remains low, as H5N1 does not currently spread between humans. State officials are emphasizing biosecurity measures and urging producers to report suspected cases promptly. The outbreak highlights the importance of monitoring wild bird migration patterns, as they can serve as vectors for the virus into domestic flocks.

Bird Flu Slams Sac County Turkey Flock, Reynolds Declares Iowa Disaster

News Brief
On October 28, 2026, Iowa confirmed its eighth HPAI outbreak of the year in a commercial turkey flock in Sac County, marking the first such case since December 2025. The flock, estimated at 44,000 to 44,400 birds, triggered an immediate disaster proclamation by Governor Kim Reynolds, effective through November 27. This follows a broader national bird flu outbreak since February 2022, with Iowa reporting 30.8 million birds lost to the virus alone. The CDC maintains that the risk to the general public remains low, as H5N1 does not currently spread between humans. State officials are emphasizing biosecurity measures and urging producers to report suspected cases promptly. The outbreak highlights the importance of monitoring wild bird migration patterns, as they can serve as vectors for the virus into domestic flocks.

Subscribe More forecasts: Atlanta 30 day forecast Greater Des Moines/ Crime & Emergencies Bird Flu Slams Sac County Turkey Flock, Reynolds Declares Iowa Disaster By Casey Santiago Published on October 29, 2026 Source: uma dantara / Unsplash Iowa's first commercial bird flu outbreak in nearly a year has landed in Sac County, where officials confirmed H5N1 highly pathogenic avian influenza in a turkey flock numbering in the tens of thousands of birds. The detection, announced Wednesday, immediately triggered a state disaster declaration and renewed warnings about biosecurity as fall bird migration ramps up across the region. State officials confirmed the case in a commercial turkey flock in Sac County on October 28, according to Iowa Agribusiness Radio Network. The flock's size is reported differently depending on the outlet: the The Des Moines Register put the number at approximately 44,000 birds, while the Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship told Iowa Capital Dispatch the flock held approximately 44,400 birds. Either way, it marks Iowa's eighth confirmed HPAI detection of 2026 and its first in a commercial operation since December 2025. Gov. Kim Reynolds responded by authorizing a disaster proclamation for Sac County, effective immediately through November 27, according to the Office of Gov. Kim Reynolds. The proclamation allows state and department resources to be deployed more easily to help contain and monitor the outbreak, the state capital newsroom reported. Outbreak response typically requires dispatching the affected flock and disposing of the dead birds, the same report notes. A Quiet Year Interrupted Before Wednesday, Iowa had reported seven 2026 outbreaks in backyard, mixed-species or game-bird flocks, according to Iowa Capital Dispatch. There had been no commercial-scale case since late last year. The last large Iowa outbreak hit in January, when a Kossuth County hatchery with roughly 25,000 pheasants, quail and chukar partridges was struck, a case also documented by the same capital newsroom. Wild birds can carry H5N1 without showing symptoms, making them an easy vector into domestic flocks, the agribusiness radio network reports. With fall migration underway, officials say biosecurity has become especially important for Iowa producers trying to keep the virus away from their barns. Iowa's Outsized Toll Iowa has had more total birds affected by bird flu than any other state since the nationwide outbreak began, according to USDA APHIS data cited by the state newsroom. The Register puts that cumulative toll at 30.8 million chickens, turkeys and other birds lost in Iowa alone, part of a national total of 210.2 million birds lost to the virus according to USDA figures. The broader outbreak has been running since February 2022 and typically intensifies each spring and fall as wild birds migrate. Iowa is far from alone this fall. APHIS has confirmed HPAI in commercial poultry and gamebird operations across five other states — Illinois, South Dakota, North Dakota, Wisconsin and Kansas — according to WATTPoultry. That included a massive flock of 934,600 egg-laying hens in Clinton County, Illinois, confirmed October 19, and a flock of 60,600 commercial meat turkeys in Dickey County, North Dakota, confirmed two days later. South Dakota alone has logged 17 affected commercial flocks so far in 2026, while a Wisconsin turkey operation of 41,400 birds and a Kansas gamebird operation of 29,000 birds rounded out the week's confirmations. What Officials Are Telling Producers and Consumers Poultry producers who suspect their birds have the virus are urged to contact a veterinarian immediately and report suspected cases to the Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship, according to the agribusiness radio network. Warning signs include sudden deaths, reduced egg production, lack of energy or appetite, respiratory problems, and swelling or discoloration around the head and legs. State officials have encouraged producers and backyard flock owners alike to keep strengthening their biosecurity measures. For consumers, officials stress that poultry and egg products remain safe to eat when properly handled and cooked to an internal temperature of 165 degrees Fahrenheit. The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention continues to assess the risk to the general public as low, and the virus is not currently known to spread between humans, according to Iowa Capital Dispatch's earlier reporting on a separate Kossuth County case. The CDC's own guidance notes that when exposure to sick or dead animals cannot be avoided, protective equipment should be worn during direct or close contact with carcasses, feces, litter or other potentially contaminated materials. H5N1 highly pathogenic avian influenza was confirmed in a commercial turkey flock in Sac County, Iowa, triggering a state disaster declaration by Kim Reynolds.

A disaster proclamation was authorized for Sac County, allowing easier deployment of state and department resources. Outbreak response typically involves dispatching the affected flock and disposing of dead birds. Officials are urging biosecurity measures and advising poultry producers to report suspected cases to the Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship.

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

犹他州停止对水貂养殖场工人进行主动监测,而西班牙也出现了禽流感 基因组 病毒 - Medical Daily

Utah Halted Active Monitoring of Mink Farm Workers While Bird Flu Genomes Show Mutation Also Seen in Spain - Medical Daily
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 28 日,犹他州停止了对水貂养殖场工人的主动监测,因为对 C&T Fenn 农场的禽流感样本进行基因测序后发现,西班牙 2022 年水貂 暴发 中也存在 突变。突变(PB1-K388R)在所有 10 个水貂样本中均被鉴定出,但在牛病毒中并未被鉴定出。犹他州官员没有计划扑杀水貂种群,理由是缺乏特定物种的权威。该州决定停止监测是基于农民的投诉,官员们表示,只有在人传人的 传播 得到确认的情况下才会恢复监测。对公众的风险仍然很低,没有证据表明在美国存在人际传播。然而,突变 引起了人们对该病毒通过在哺乳动物中反复传播而可能适应的担忧。这些发现是初步的,报告并未表明 突变 会增加对人类的危险。本报告强调了各州对 H5N1 暴发 的不同反应,以及该病毒在不同 宿主 物种中行为的持续不确定性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
在禽流感 基因组 中发现 突变 后,犹他州于 2026 年 10 月 28 日停止了对水貂养殖场工人的主动监测。突变 在 2026 年 8 月收集的样本中被鉴定出来,也在西班牙 2022 年的水貂 暴发 中被发现。报告发布日期为2026年10月29日。
地点与对象
该事件发生在犹他州,特别是在位于盐湖城以南约一小时车程的 C&T Fenn 农场。受影响人群包括水貂养殖场工人及其家人,以及养殖场的水貂种群。
事件
对犹他州水貂养殖场的禽流感基因测序显示,每个样本中都含有 突变,这在西班牙 2022 年水貂中也出现了 暴发。由于抱怨烦扰,犹他州官员停止了对农场工人的主动监控,该州也没有计划扑杀水貂。这是一份初步的基因组报告,并未证实 突变 对人类健康的影响。
规模、影响与风险
在所有 10 个水貂样本中都发现了 突变 PB1-K388R,但在牛病毒中没有发现。公众面临的风险仍然很低,在美国尚未发现人际传播。人们关注的焦点是工人及其家人,以及在哺乳动物中的反复传播是否会导致病毒的适应性增强。
应对、状态与不确定性
犹他州农业官员口头禁止将动物和材料运出农场,但没有计划扑杀。该州已停止主动监测农场工人,只有在有证据表明存在人传人 传播 的情况下才会恢复监测。这些调查结果是初步的,对公众的风险被认为很低。该报告没有提供 突变 对人类健康影响的决定性证据。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Utah Halted Active Monitoring of Mink Farm Workers While Bird Flu Genomes Show Mutation Also Seen in Spain - Medical Daily

News Brief
On October 28, 2026, Utah halted active monitoring of mink farm workers after genetic sequencing of bird flu samples from C&T Fenn Farms revealed a mutation also seen in Spain's 2022 mink outbreak. The mutation, PB1-K388R, was identified in all 10 mink samples but not in cattle viruses. Utah officials have not planned to cull the mink population, citing a lack of species-specific authority. The state's decision to stop monitoring was based on complaints from farmers, and officials stated it would resume only if human-to-human transmission is confirmed. The risk to the general public remains low, with no evidence of person-to-person spread in the U.S. However, the mutation raises concerns about the virus's potential adaptation through repeated spread in mammals. The findings are preliminary, and the report does not indicate the mutation increases danger to humans. This report highlights the varied state responses to H5N1 outbreaks and the ongoing uncertainty surrounding the virus's behavior in different host species.

Conditions Utah Halted Active Monitoring of Mink Farm Workers While Bird Flu Genomes Show Mutation Also Seen in Spain Published Oct 28, 2026 10:55 PM EDT By Elena Vega MADS CLAUS RASMUSSEN/Ritzau Scanpix | AFP via Getty Images Share on Twitter Share on Facebook Share on WhatsApp Genetic sequencing of bird flu from a Utah mink farm found a mutation in every sample that is rarely seen in wild birds and also appeared in Spain's 2022 mink outbreak, according to a preliminary genomic report posted Oct. 22, as Nature World News reported. Utah officials have not culled the animals and have stopped actively monitoring farm workers. The findings arrive as Washington confirms its first H5N1 case tied to a dairy worker. Together, the two outbreaks show the virus moving through mammals in the West, and state responses that vary widely. The risk to the general public remains low, health officials say, and no person-to-person spread has been found in the United States. The concern centers on workers and their families, and on whether repeated spread in mammals gives the virus more chances to adapt. Developing Story Note: This report covers new developments involving H5N1 bird flu in Utah mink and Washington dairy cattle. Information may change as authorities, researchers or public health agencies release additional findings. Latest verified update checked Oct. 28, 2026; the most recent genome data cover samples collected Aug. 7. Mink Genomes and Utah's Hands-Off Response Researchers from the National Institutes of Health, Britain's Pirbright Institute and France's CNRS analyzed 10 virus genomes from C&T Fenn Farms, about an hour's drive south of Salt Lake City. The virus belongs to the B3.13 genotype that has circulated in U.S. dairy cattle since 2024, and its closest relatives were Texas cattle samples from late May. All 10 mink samples carried a change called PB1-K388R, which was not found in the cattle viruses. What the mutation does is poorly understood, and the report does not show it makes the virus more dangerous to people. The analysis points to a single introduction around early July that spread undetected for weeks. Utah agriculture officials placed a verbal hold on moving animals and materials off the farm but have no plans to cull. "There's just not a lot of options out there," State Veterinarian Amanda Price told ProPublica, which reported that Spain and Finland depopulated infected fur farms. The USDA said it has no species-specific authority or dedicated program to cull, vaccinate, or compensate mink owners. The state also stopped actively monitoring farm workers after agriculture officials complained that farmers were annoyed by it, ProPublica found. A respiratory disease epidemiologist with the state health department said monitoring would resume only if there was evidence of human-to-human transmission. Reporters saw a worker with a visibly red eye, a common bird flu symptom, who was not tested, and the farm owner attributed it to dust. Washington Dairy Case and the Wider Mammal Picture MedicalDaily previously reported that H5N1 has reached eight Yakima County dairy herds and infected a dairy worker with mild eye and respiratory symptoms. The CDC confirmed the virus as H5N1 in its weekly FluView report published Oct. 23, the first H5 case in Washington linked to a dairy worker. The report states that human-to-human transmission of H5 bird flu "has not been identified in the United States." Sequences from the Washington herds have not been released, so it is not yet known whether they carry the same genotype or mutations as Utah's mink. The CDC has counted 72 human H5 cases since February 2024, according to its bird flu situation summary, last updated Oct. 15, with cases reported among dairy and poultry workers. Mink worry scientists for a specific reason. They are highly susceptible to both bird flu and seasonal human flu, which creates a setting where the viruses could swap genes. With estimated flu hospitalization rates already at their highest mid-October level since the 2010-11 season, CDC guidance that workers exposed to animals get seasonal flu shots carries added weight. The global link is concrete. Spain's 2022 outbreak carried the same PB1 change, and Spain and Finland responded by culling. The report's authors point to large surveillance gaps, which make it harder to judge whether the virus is changing. Farm Families, Raw Milk and Reasonable Precautions People who work with dairy cattle, mink, poultry, or other sick animals face the highest risk. Eye redness, cough, sore throat, fever, or body aches after animal contact should prompt a call to a clinician, mentioning the exposure, and the CDC urges testing even for mild symptoms like a red eye. Prescription flu antivirals work best when started early, so exposed workers should not wait to see if symptoms pass. Workers who fear losing pay or immigration trouble may avoid testing, and local health departments can answer questions about testing and protective gear. Eye protection, masks, and gloves reduce exposure, and family members of farm workers can ask local health departments about testing if someone at home becomes sick. For most households, pasteurized milk and dairy remain safe. Raw milk can carry the virus and should be avoided, according to the CDC's bird flu prevention guidance, and pets should be kept away from raw pet food and sick or dead birds. Households with no farm contact face low risk. Key questions remain open. The researchers judged frozen feed an unlikely source, but how the virus reached the farm is still unknown. The report's authors said they knew of no testing of nearby farms or wildlife, and officials have not said whether any Utah worker was infected or whether the virus has changed since August. Developing Story Timeline Oct. 23, 2026: ProPublica reported that Utah stopped actively monitoring mink farm workers and has no plan to cull. A worker with a red eye was not tested. Oct. 23, 2026: The CDC's FluView confirmed the Washington dairy worker's virus as H5N1. Oct. 22, 2026: Researchers posted a preliminary report on 10 Utah mink genomes. All carried the PB1-K388R mutation also seen in Spain. Oct. 15, 2026: Yakima Health District announced the human case. The worker was isolating at home and was not severely ill. Aug. 17, 2026: The USDA confirmed H5N1 on the Utah mink farm, the first detection in U.S. farmed mink. Samples had been collected Aug. 7. H5N1 of the B3.13 dairy cattle genotype, with a PB1-K388R mutation in all 10 sequenced samples that also appeared in Spain's 2022 mink outbreak. Does the mutation make bird flu dangerous to people? B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

禽流感正在水貂养殖场肆虐。专家称,这可能引发人类大流行,但官员们不会采取行动。

Bird flu is raging on a mink farm. It could spark a human pandemic, experts say, but officials won’t act.
新闻简报ProPublica 的一项调查显示,犹他州的一个水貂养殖场出现了不受控制的 暴发 禽流感疫情,引发了人们对人际传播 传播 病毒和大流行的可能性的担忧。暴发持续了两个多月,涉及数千只水貂,它们的肺部和呼吸道与人类相似,使其成为潜在的中间体宿主。尽管存在风险,州和联邦官员却没有采取任何行动,将毛皮贸易利益置于公共卫生之上。已有两人死于受感染水貂感染的禽流感,但尚未证实人与人之间的传播。专家警告说,单个突变可能会让病毒更容易感染人类,但官方尚未实施监测或扑杀措施。疾病预防控制中心敦促对眼睛发红等症状进行检测,这是感染的常见症状,但犹他州卫生官员尚未按照这一指导采取行动。缺乏联邦监督和未能监控工人被流感专家批评为“不负责任”和“令人费解”。
查看新闻五要素
时间
暴发 已在犹他州的一家水貂养殖场肆虐了两个多月,首次报告于 8 月中旬发布,截至 2026 年 10 月,人们的担忧仍在持续。
地点与对象
暴发 事件发生在犹他州的 C&T Fenn 农场,该农场饲养了数千只水貂用于毛皮加工。水貂工人,包括讲西班牙语的农场工人,都面临着暴露的风险。
事件
犹他州的一家水貂养殖场正在发生禽流感 暴发(暴发)疫情,专家们对 传播 病毒人传人的可能性以及引发大流行的风险表示担忧。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 已杀死两名从患病动物身上感染病毒的人。该病毒尚未被证明可以在人与人之间传播,但专家警告说,单个 突变 就可能改变这种情况。由于水貂与人类的生物学相似性及其攻击性行为,跨物种溢出 对人类的风险被认为很大。
应对、状态与不确定性
尽管有疾病预防控制中心的指导,犹他州官员尚未采取主动措施来监测或扑杀受感染的水貂。该州的政策重点是允许农民决定是否联系卫生官员,并且没有针对水貂养殖场的联邦法规。情况仍未解决,专家称这种反应“不负责任”且“令人费解”。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu is raging on a mink farm. It could spark a human pandemic, experts say, but officials won’t act.

News Brief
A ProPublica investigation reveals an uncontrolled outbreak of bird flu on a mink farm in Utah, raising concerns about the potential for human-to-human transmission and a pandemic. The outbreak, which has lasted over two months, involves thousands of mink, whose lungs and airways are similar to humans, making them a potential intermediate host. Despite the risk, state and federal officials have taken little action, prioritizing the fur trade interests over public health. Two people have died from bird flu contracted from infected mink, but no human-to-human spread has been confirmed. Experts warn that a single mutation could allow the virus to infect humans more easily, but officials have not implemented monitoring or culling measures. The CDC urges testing for symptoms like eye redness, which is a common sign of infection, but Utah health officials have not acted on this guidance. The lack of federal oversight and the failure to monitor workers have been criticized as "irresponsible" and "puzzling" by flu experts.
Original source text (English)
Aster, a mink rescued from a fur farm. Julie Leopo for ProPublica See all topics This story was originally published by ProPublica , a nonprofit newsroom that investigates abuses of power. Sign up to receive their biggest stories as soon as they’re published. The strain of bird flu that’s ripped through American barns for the last four years has killed two people who caught it from sick animals. But as far as anyone can tell, humans can’t spread it. The virus has remained a true “bird” flu, tailored to attach to avian cells, not invade human throats and noses, where coughing and sneezing reliably spew droplets. Under the right conditions, however, that could change. A single mutation could give the bird flu a foothold in people and spark a pandemic that experts fear could be deadlier than COVID-19. For that to happen, this bird flu would likely need to find an intermediate host — an animal vulnerable to viruses that infect both birds and humans. These animals would be crowded together, each infection raising the odds that a new strain could arise through mutation. Then, for that strain to jump across species, it would have to have access to humans and time to spread among them undetected. That perfect petri dish exists right now, a ProPublica investigation found. For over two months, an outbreak of bird flu has raged uncontrolled on a Utah farm that raises thousands of mink for fur. Mink have lungs and airways that on a cellular level are remarkably similar to those of people; they have caught and spread other flus and infected some humans with COVID-19. Instead of trying to stop the outbreak and prevent it from spilling into humans, state and federal officials have done little except try to keep its existence quiet, our investigation shows. The failure to act amounts to a cascade of missteps flu experts called “irresponsible” and “puzzling.” One called it “playing with fire.” Utah agriculture officials learned in mid-August that mink were dying of bird flu on C&T Fenn Farms, about an hour’s drive south from Salt Lake City, according to communications released under the state’s public records law. C&T Fenn Farms, a mink fur farm west of Provo, Utah, on Sept. 25. Julie Leopo for ProPublica Had poultry been infected, the playbook would have been clear: The strain is so virulent that if a single chicken tests positive, the U.S. government requires farmers to kill the whole flock to protect other farms as well as public health. Poultry farmers are reimbursed for the loss. But there are no federal rules for how to deal with sick mink. Handling Utah’s response was state veterinarian Amanda Price, who sought help from the U.S. Department of Agriculture, which takes the lead on bird flu outbreaks involving poultry and cows. USDA officials said they didn’t regulate mink or have a vaccine that was approved to give them. And though officials with the Centers for Disease Control and Prevention spoke with Price’s health department counterparts who were less involved with the outbreak, they never talked to her, and she never reached out. The threat of spillover has been far from the minds of Utah officials, records and interviews show. Instead, front and center have been the interests of a business raising animals to skin for luxury pelts to ship overseas. “It has been devastating,” Price told ProPublica of the outbreak and its impact on the farm’s owner. “This is his life’s work that he’s seeing be destroyed. I really hope that you understand the humanity behind this situation.” Flu experts were unequivocal that the government should have ordered the farm to cull its mink, as other countries have done . But Utah never seriously considered it. A fur trade representative warned Price that doing so would kill the business and, ultimately, the industry. Farms spend decades breeding mink to develop unique genetic lineages. “There’s just not a lot of options out there other than kind of sitting and waiting and seeing what happens,” Price said, noting that USDA officials did not urge her to kill the mink or make her feel like bird flu in mink was any riskier than infections in other animals that have caught it, like ducks and cows. Flu experts found that response surprising. “Every other country that I’m aware of that has had H5N1 in fur farms, depopulation has happened pretty quickly,” said Tom Peacock, an avian influenza researcher at the Pirbright Institute. “The fact that the first instinct wasn’t just to depopulate and figure stuff out later is very concerning to me.” And all the while, officials didn’t monitor the mink workers for symptoms, as guidance from the CDC urges. Their exposure is significant: Mink sneeze. They escape. They attack. “The mink are fierce,” said a Spanish-speaking “minkero” who works on a nearby farm, showing the scars he got from the aggressive, solitary creatures. “You get scared because the fucking thing bites, and it claws at you.” Right now in Washington state, officials are working to track down the contacts of a symptomatic person exposed to dairy cattle infected with bird flu. The patient is isolating at home. Utah public health officials used to do similar, proactive monitoring. But they stopped after complaints from their colleagues in the agriculture department that farmers were annoyed by it. “Us doing active monitoring was making it so that farms didn’t want to work with them,” said Pamela Gomez Pinedo, a respiratory disease epidemiologist for Utah’s health department. As a result, public health workers had a “pretty difficult time” getting details from agriculture officials, she said. A mink farmworker, sometimes referred to as a “minkero” among the Spanish-speaking community, shows scars from mink bites and scratches in Payson, Utah. Workers typically wear long gloves to protect themselves but can still be bitten or scratched. Julie Leopo for ProPublica As part of a 2025 agreement , the Utah Department of Agriculture and Food agreed to share information about bird flu outbreaks on farms — so long as the health officials didn’t do anything to track workers’ health. “We will only do active monitoring in the case of evidence of human-to-human transmission,” Pinedo said in emails with a CDC epidemiologist. As the outbreak stretched to a month, CDC officials expressed concerns, asking how anyone would know if a worker had been infected if they weren’t getting tested. Price said the state’s policy not to monitor workers is meant to allow farmers to decide whether to contact the health department, rather than “forcing it on them.” “If we started seeing people actively dying from this, obviously that would change how we respond to this,” she said. A telltale sign of bird flu infection in humans is eye redness. It is present in over 90% of cases and is often the sole symptom a person displays. Last Saturday, a ProPublica reporter tracked down two people who worked at the Utah mink farm. One had a visibly red eye. The CDC explicitly urges testing for the symptom . After ProPublica reached out to the farm, its owner, Chad Fenn, contacted the Utah County Health Department about the red eye, which had cleared up by Monday, he told ProPublica. He said the worker got dust in his eye; the department didn’t recommend testing. “They’re not going to do anything,” Fenn said. “They said they don’t see that there’s a need.” He said the department recommended testing only if other symptoms, like a cough or sore throat, developed. A half dozen flu experts had no shortage of adjectives to describe their disbelief at the government decisions not to cull the mink or monitor and test the workers. “It’s amazing to me,” said Gail Hansen, a former Kansas state public health veterinarian and epidemiologist. “Utah doesn’t want to be the epicenter of the next human flu outbreak, right?” Most of the new viruses that have shaken humanity in the past century have spilled over from animals. Ebolas likely came from fruit bats; HIV, from chimpanzees. The leading theory for COVID-19 is that it jumped from bats to humans via an intermediate host mammal such as a raccoon dog. And all the modern flu pandemics have been bird flu viruses that mutated to spread in humans. ProPublica reported extensively on spillover in a 2023 series called “Roots of an Outbreak.” To prevent more deadly spillovers, experts worldwide urge governments to take a “ one health ” approach to viral threats, treating dangers to animals as dangers to humans. That’s not happening in Utah, experts said after reviewing ProPublica’s findings. Both the state and federal government are to blame. “There’s no leader of this ‘one health’ team to take charge,” said Erin Sorrell, a senior scholar at the Johns Hopkins Center for Health Security. “That is going to allow the virus to continue to circulate, potentially adapt and potentially spill over into another host, whether it’s avian, mammalian or human.” This strain of bird flu, which began ripping through American poultry farms in early 2022, has proved adept at jumping to other species. It took hold in cows in 2024 and since then has run rampant through dairies . Yet federal guidance to prevent the spread into mink farms hasn’t considered cows a threat. Farmers are encouraged to keep wild birds from infiltrating their barns and avoid feeding them raw poultry. It has said nothing about feeding beef parts to mink, which farmers commonly do in summer months to encourage the animals to put on weight and grow. Dairy cows, which are not “depopulated” if exposed to bird flu, can still be slaughtered with the meat sold to farms and humans even though researchers have found the virus in the tissues of sick cows . USDA officials have insisted that beef for human consumption is free of bird flu , in part because the virus concentrates itself in sections of the cow people don’t usually eat. Fenn said that the mink are routinely fed raw organs of livestock, including cows. The genomic data from the samples of mink lungs and brains sent from Utah to a USDA lab shows that the mink were stricken with the same strain of the virus that has spread in dairy herds. Officials said they didn’t know whether contaminated beef was the way the mink got infected. Cows graze in a field in Utah Valley, Utah. Julie Leopo for ProPublica In mink, the dairy strain of bird flu may have found a much better host for mutating into a human flu, because mink airways have an abundance of the type of cell receptors in people’s throats and noses that are a common pathway for infection. A 2024 study of the dairy strain of the bird flu found that a single mutation could switch the virus from preferring the bird-type receptors to human-type receptors. And in studies using ferrets , a close relative of mink, the virus acquired mutations that let it attack human-type cell receptors. It’s not clear to scientists just how much of an evolutionary leap would be needed for this strain of bird flu to spread among humans, or whether mink are the host that would allow that leap to happen. How this ultimately plays out “really depends on what happens with the virus,” said Meghan Davis, an epidemiologist at Johns Hopkins University. “We might get lucky, and a response that is not robust may not have dire consequences,” she said. “But we may get unlucky.” There’s also a risk of something called “reassortment,” where another flu virus could infect the same cell as a bird flu virus and swap wide swaths of genetic code already adapted to humans. When the outbreak in the mink farm started, there was very little seasonal flu it could mix with, but September saw one of the earliest starts to flu season on record , compared with November, when flu normally takes off. “The most likely scenario for a pandemic is that we have a virus that reassorts with another virus,” said Jennifer Nuzzo, director of the Pandemic Center at the Brown University School of Public Health. “Obviously, having this circulate amid human flu season is not great.” Utah officials were far more responsive to the threat of spillover in 2020, when outbreaks of COVID-19 spread explosively on mink farms – including this very farm. They invited federal officials to the state to research how the virus adapted and moved. Given mink’s reputation as escape artists, wildlife experts set up traps around infected farms. The traps frequently caught mink carrying COVID-19 that had originated in the nearby barns. The virus infected both escaped mink and wild mink they had come in contact with . Mink and farmworkers were frequently tested, tracking the way that the virus was spreading and mutating . As other states and countries took the same measures, they identified humans infected by the mink with COVID-19 and, ultimately, human-to-human transmission. There are no similar efforts now in Utah with bird flu. A USDA spokesperson told ProPublica that it was more involved in 2020 because the virus that causes COVID-19 had “significant” potential to transmit between humans and animals and vice versa. Scientists with the USDA sequenced and published bird flu genomic data from the first samples of dead mink. Those sequences didn’t show any particularly concerning mutations, experts and the CDC told ProPublica. But in the past two months, experts said, the virus is sure to have continued mutating as it spread – it’s only a question of whether those mutations could confer some advantage in infecting humans as well. “As long as it continues to go mink to mink, then it becomes more and more likely that it could jump to a human,” Hansen said. Fenn said new samples were taken two weeks ago. Price said she is still waiting for the results. Mink have the type of cell receptors that a seasonal flu will target in human throats and noses. Julie Leopo for ProPublica John Korslund, a retired USDA official who helped coordinate the response to COVID-19 on mink farms several years prior, was one of several experts who said officials need to look closely at newer genetic samples to see how the virus is evolving. “I really want to know what the virus has done, from when it started to today,” he said. “Are we getting accumulated mutations making the virus more dangerous?” ProPublica began reporting on the outbreak late this summer after the USDA updated a bird flu dashboard that showed seven captive mink had tested positive in Utah. But neither the USDA nor state officials had said anything publicly, and, when asked, they wouldn’t discuss what was actually happening on the farm. Communications obtained under the state’s public records law — first published by the Animal Welfare Institute and reported on by the Salt Lake Tribune — show Utah officials took pains to protect the farm’s privacy amid the outbreak. The state did not declare an emergency , because doing so, officials said, “may alert animal rights groups,” according to the messages. Willy Lanier, a public health veterinarian with the state health department, told colleagues to keep the outbreak under “closer hold” than outbreaks of bird flu on poultry and dairy farms. Keeping the information confidential was consistent with state protocol, a spokesperson in his department said, adding that it was still shared with local health officials. But when ProPublica reached out to the spokesperson for the Utah County Health Department about a possible bird flu outbreak, she said, “That would be news to me.” Price is so deferential to the family that owns the farm that she has not set foot on the property. “I’m really the only one here at this facility that’s been dealing with the producer. They’re a very private bunch,” Price said. “A lot of what I’m going off of is what I’ve seen from Google Maps, what I’ve talked to the producer about, what I’ve talked to the vet about. I haven’t felt the need to go out there based on those conversations.” She said she “became very comfortable” in conversations with the owner that the virus wouldn’t spread off the farm. It doesn’t have animals moving on and off over the course of the year like on a dairy or poultry farm; mink leave only when they’re slaughtered and skinned for pelts at the end of the year. She also said that she was confident the owner would recapture any escaped mink. Short of a formal quarantine, Price gave the farm a “verbal hold” to not move any mink, or manure or carcasses, off the farm. “We haven’t seen spread off of this farm. We have not had additional cases in Utah that are linked to this, so thus far the verbal hold orders and the measures that have been taken look to be successful: There is not spread happening from this farm from this case.” Price’s assertion didn’t allay the concerns of experts that ProPublica spoke with. When poultry test positive for this strain of bird flu, all nearby farms are quarantined and tested for the virus. But the approach in Utah relies on farms reporting cases or the public calling in reports of dead birds in the area. “If you’re not looking for something, you won’t find it,” Sorrell said. “Unless you’re doing active surveillance, you can’t claim that you haven’t had cases.” Fenn told ProPublica his conversations with state officials made him less worried about any kind of spread. When the outbreak started, Fenn said, he asked whether the bird flu could infect people. “Right off the bat, the state people we reported to, they said you don’t need to worry too much,” Fenn said. “My guys wear masks every day. They have glasses they can wear,” Fenn said. “ Me? I don’t wear it at all. Never have, never will, because I’ve been doing it for 40 years.” Fenn said he wished he could vaccinate the mink for bird flu to keep the rest of his animals from catching it and dying; Price asked the USDA about a vaccine that had been used to save endangered wild species but said she got nowhere with that request. The USDA told ProPublica its division responsible for protecting animal health has no “species-specific regulatory authority or dedicated programs” for vacc...

Time
The outbreak has raged on a Utah mink farm for over two months, with the first reports in mid-August and ongoing concerns as of October 2026.
Location and population
The outbreak occurred at C&T Fenn Farms in Utah, which raises thousands of mink for fur. Mink workers, including Spanish-speaking farm laborers, are at risk of exposure.
Event
An outbreak of bird flu (avian influenza) has been ongoing at a mink farm in Utah, with concerns raised by experts about the potential for human-to-human transmission and the risk of sparking a pandemic.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak has killed two people who caught the virus from sick animals. The virus has not been shown to spread between humans, but experts warn that a single mutation could change this. The risk of spillover to humans is considered significant due to the mink's biological similarity to humans and their aggressive behavior.
Response, status and uncertainty
Utah officials have not taken proactive measures to monitor or cull the infected mink, despite CDC guidance. The state's policy focuses on allowing farmers to decide whether to contact health officials, and there is no federal regulation for mink farms. The situation remains unresolved, with experts calling the response "irresponsible" and "puzzling.".

H5N1 病理学揭示家禽器官损伤的物种特异性模式 - aviNews

H5N1 Pathology Reveals Species-Specific Patterns of Organ Damage in Poultry - aviNews
新闻简报2026 年《家禽科学》上发表的一项研究调查了台湾家禽中自然发生的 H5N1 感染,检查了多个物种的病理变化。这项研究在 41 个 H5N1 阳性农场(23 个鸡、4 个鸭、10 个鹅、4 个鹌鹑)中进行,揭示了器官损伤和细胞反应的物种特异性模式。这些差异强调了依靠单一可见病变或症状来诊断 HPAI 感染的局限性。该研究结合了大体病理学、组织病理学、免疫组织化学和细胞凋亡分析,以更好地了解该疾病的影响。它强调需要进行结构化调查,考虑 宿主 物种、临床病史、受影响的器官和实验室检查结果。研究结果并未建立所有 H5N1 感染的通用模式,但强化了将物种特异性病理学与 监测 和诊断相结合的价值。该研究还表明,人工智能等诊断技术的未来进步可以增强疾病监测和解释。

规模、影响与风险

B routine priorityPublic-health news

H5N1 Pathology Reveals Species-Specific Patterns of Organ Damage in Poultry - aviNews

News Brief
A 2026 study published in Poultry Science investigated naturally occurring H5N1 infections in poultry in Taiwan, examining pathological changes across multiple species. The research, conducted on 41 H5N1-positive farms (23 chicken, 4 duck, 10 goose, 4 quail), revealed species-specific patterns of organ damage and cellular responses. These differences underscore the limitations of relying on a single visible lesion or symptom for diagnosing HPAI infections. The study combined gross pathology, histopathology, immunohistochemistry, and apoptosis analysis to better understand the disease's impact. It highlights the need for a structured investigation that considers host species, clinical history, affected organs, and laboratory findings. The findings do not establish a universal pattern for all H5N1 infections but reinforce the value of integrating species-specific pathology with surveillance and diagnostics. The research also suggests that future advancements in diagnostic technologies, such as artificial intelligence, could enhance disease monitoring and interpretation.

READ SUMMARY SUBSCRIBE RECEIVE IN PAPER OTHER EDITIONS Back Animal Health & Pathology 29 Oct 2026 H5N1 Pathology Reveals Species-Specific Patterns of Organ Damage in Poultry Highly pathogenic avian influenza (H5N1) can produce different patterns of organ damage across poultry species. Understanding these differences is essential [...] Sahar Darazi SAVE IN THE LIBRARY PDF FILE Highly pathogenic avian influenza (H5N1) can produce different patterns of organ damage across poultry species. Understanding these differences is essential for improving disease diagnosis, pathological investigations, and surveillance. POULTRY HEALTH & PATHOLOGY A Global Poultry-Health Challenge Highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI), particularly the H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b lineage, remains a major concern for poultry production and animal health worldwide. Beyond its rapid spread and potential to cause substantial mortality, understanding how the virus damages different organs is essential for improving disease diagnosis and surveillance. A 2026 study published in Poultry Science investigated naturally occurring H5N1 infections in poultry in Taiwan, examining pathological changes across multiple species. The findings provide insights into how the same viral lineage can produce different disease patterns depending on the host species (1). KEY SCIENTIFIC FINDING Continue after advertising. The same H5N1 viral lineage can produce different patterns of organ damage and cellular responses across poultry species. Different Species, Different Pathological Responses Researchers examined samples from 41 H5N1-positive farms, including 23 chicken farms, four duck farms, ten goose farms, and four quail farms. The investigation combined gross pathology, histopathology, immunohistochemistry, and analyses of cellular apoptosis. The researchers evaluated tissues from several organs, including the brain, heart, liver, spleen, lungs, kidneys, and pancreas. CHICKENS Widespread systemic infection, extensive tissue necrosis, and viral antigen distribution. DUCKS Generally milder lesions and a more coordinated apoptotic response. GEESE Intermediate pathological characteristics. QUAILS A distinct pattern involving prominent splenic lesions and different relationships between viral distribution, cell death, and tissue damage. These differences demonstrate why clinical signs or examination of a single organ may not fully characterize the severity and distribution of HPAI infection (1). Why Diagnostic Pathology Matters Gross examination can identify visible abnormalities, including haemorrhages, organ enlargement, and tissue discoloration. However, these findings may overlap with those associated with other infectious diseases. Histopathology allows veterinarians to examine tissue architecture and cellular injury under a microscope. Immunohistochemistry helps identify viral antigens within affected tissues, allowing investigators to assess viral distribution. The study also investigated apoptosis, the regulated process of cell death. Comparing apoptotic signals with tissue lesions and viral antigen distribution helps clarify how infection and host responses contribute to organ damage. A review of avian diagnostic pathology emphasizes the potential value of combining conventional pathological examination with molecular diagnostics, immunohistochemistry, and digital pathology. Artificial intelligence and machine learning may further support diagnostic interpretation and disease surveillance as these technologies develop (2). Implications for Poultry Disease Surveillance Pathological severity and viral distribution are not necessarily identical across poultry species. Diagnostic investigations should consider the host species, clinical history, affected organs, and laboratory findings rather than relying on one visible lesion or symptom. For poultry veterinarians and diagnostic laboratories, a structured investigation combining necropsy, tissue examination, and confirmatory laboratory testing can support more accurate characterization of suspected outbreaks. These findings do not establish a universal pathological pattern for every H5N1 infection. The study was based on naturally infected farms in Taiwan, and its results should be interpreted within that epidemiological context. Nevertheless, the research strengthens the case for integrating species-specific pathology with laboratory confirmation and surveillance to improve understanding of HPAI and other emerging poultry diseases. Scientific References (1) Yi, Z.-J., Huang, Y.-I., Lin, Y.-J., Tsai, Y.-W., Cheng, M.-C., & Chen, Y.-M. (2026). Comprehensive pathological, immunohistochemical, and apoptotic analysis of the highly pathogenic avian influenza A virus H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b in poultry in Taiwan. Poultry Science. Read the scientific study → (2) Getnet, G. M., Getnet, M. A., & Atenaf, A. S. (2026). Advances in Avian Diagnostic Pathology: Current Trends, Challenges and Future Directions: A Review. Veterinary Medicine and Science, 12(3), e70905. avian influenza dairy biosecurity | Washington dairy bird flu cases triple to 27 as H5N1 spreads - https://en.edairynews.com/

News Brief
The news report from eDairyNews dated 2026-10-28 highlights a significant increase in bird flu cases in Washington, United States, with the number tripling to 27 as the H5N1 virus spreads. The report notes the spread of the virus but does not provide specific case or death counts. The situation is under investigation, and no detailed response measures are mentioned. The article also discusses broader dairy industry topics, including production, policy, and market trends, but the focus on H5N1 bird flu indicates a growing concern for the region's dairy farms and bird populations. The implications of this spread are not fully elaborated, but the report underscores the need for vigilance and potential biosecurity measures to mitigate further impact.
Original source text (English)

B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

通用流感 疫苗 平台向更广泛的 H5N1 大流行做好准备 - Bioengineer.org

Universal influenza vaccine platforms advance toward broader H5N1 pandemic readiness - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报2026 年 10 月 4 日在《Discover Immunity》上发表的一篇评论强调了通用流感 疫苗 平台在更广泛的 H5N1 大流行准备方面取得的进展。该审查强调,H5N1(一种 高致病性禽流感 病毒)由于其抗原变异性和人传人 传播 的潜力而构成重大威胁。它描述了当前的 疫苗(例如灭活和重组血凝素 疫苗)如何具有菌株特异性并且缺乏真实世界的有效性数据。该评论还讨论了下一代 疫苗 的开发,包括 mRNA 和病毒样颗粒平台,该平台提供更快的生产和更广泛的 免疫应答。然而,它指出,这些 疫苗 在诱导针对多种 H5N1 变种的持久、交叉保护性免疫力方面仍然面临挑战。作者强调需要进一步研究和开发,以提高 疫苗 功效并解决 H5N1 快速 突变 速率和扩大的 宿主 范围带来的风险。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该审查于 2026 年 10 月 4 日发布,活动日期为 2026 年 10 月 28 日。该审查讨论了截至 2026 年 10 月与 H5N1 相关的持续进展和担忧。
地点与对象
此次审查的范围是全球性的,重点关注H5N1 禽流感,该病毒已在家禽中引起暴发,并在各个地区引起野生鸟类。它还提到了人类感染,特别是家禽工人和暴露于受污染环境的个人之间的感染。
事件
2026 年 10 月 4 日在《Discover Immunity》上发表的一篇评论研究了通用流感 疫苗 平台在更广泛的 H5N1 大流行准备方面的进展。该综述强调了开发针对 H5N1 的有效 疫苗 所面临的挑战,因为它的抗原变异性和缺乏真实世界的有效性数据。
规模、影响与风险
H5N1 禽流感 被描述为一种特别危险的毒株,对人类具有很高的死亡率。它会导致家禽周期性 暴发 和全球 野生鸟类,导致重大经济损失。人类感染主要通过与受感染的鸡或受污染的环境密切接触而发生,突变 导致人与人之间持续 传播 的风险仍然是一个主要问题。
应对、状态与不确定性
该综述讨论了当前的 疫苗 平台,包括灭活和重组血凝素 疫苗,并强调了向 mRNA 和病毒样颗粒 疫苗 等新平台的转变。它指出,虽然这些 疫苗 显示出希望,但缺乏现实世界的有效性数据,需要进一步的研究和开发来优化它们以实现更广泛的保护。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Universal influenza vaccine platforms advance toward broader H5N1 pandemic readiness - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
A review published in Discover Immunity on 4 October 2026 highlights the progress of universal influenza vaccine platforms toward broader H5N1 pandemic readiness. The review emphasizes that H5N1, a highly pathogenic avian influenza virus, is a significant threat due to its antigenic variability and potential for human-to-human transmission. It describes how current vaccines, such as inactivated and recombinant hemagglutinin vaccines, are strain-specific and lack real-world effectiveness data. The review also discusses the development of next-generation vaccines, including mRNA and virus-like particle platforms, which offer faster production and broader immune responses. However, it notes that these vaccines still face challenges in inducing durable, cross-protective immunity against multiple H5N1 variants. The authors stress the need for further research and development to improve vaccine efficacy and address the risks posed by H5N1's rapid mutation rate and expanded host range.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, October 30, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Health Universal influenza vaccine platforms advance toward broader H5N1 pandemic readiness by October 28, 2026 in Health Reading Time: 7 mins read 0 Universal influenza vaccine platforms advance toward broader H5N1 pandemic readiness Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram A newly published review in Discover Immunity examines how universal influenza vaccine platforms could strengthen the world’s defenses against H5N1 avian influenza, a virus the authors describe as a significant threat to global public health. The review, published on 4 October 2026 as an open-access article, brings together current evidence on licensed and experimental H5N1 vaccines, the immunological factors that shape their performance, and the manufacturing and distribution systems that would determine whether an effective response to an outbreak is even possible. Its central argument is that protection against a virus as antigenically variable as H5N1 cannot rest on strain-matched vaccines alone, but requires platforms built around conserved regions of the virus that can generate durable, cross-protective immunity against multiple variants. The review begins by situating H5N1 within the broader landscape of zoonotic threats. H5N1 avian influenza is described as a particularly dangerous strain of influenza A virus because it can infect both birds and humans. First identified in Hong Kong in 1997, the virus has since caused periodic outbreaks in poultry and wild birds across global regions, with occasional human infections that have had fatal consequences. Highly pathogenic avian influenza viruses kill large quantities of poultry and impose significant economic losses on the poultry industry worldwide. In humans, the review notes, H5N1 infection can lead to acute respiratory distress and organ failure, with a fatality rate described as extremely high compared with seasonal influenza strains. Human infections occur mainly through close contact with infected chickens or contaminated environments, and the possibility that mutations could enable sustained human-to-human transmission remains a central concern. The virus’s rapid mutation rate and its potential for human-to-human spread are cited as the reasons it has become a priority for global health surveillance and preparedness. Against this background, the review assesses what current H5N1 vaccine platforms actually deliver. Licensed, prepandemic and stockpiled H5N1 vaccines are inactivated and recombinant hemagglutinin vaccines, developed with or without adjuvants. The immunogenicity literature indicates that adjuvanted preparations consistently induce higher hemagglutination inhibition and neutralizing antibody titers than non-adjuvanted preparations, and reach seroprotective titers, defined as an HAI titer of at least 1:40, with lower antigen doses and fewer injections. Oil-in-water adjuvants such as AS03 and MF59 stimulate B cell responses to antigens and engage T follicular helper cells, increasing the extent and persistence of humoral immune responses. Strain-specific neutralization is strong with inactivated adjuvanted vaccines when the vaccine matches the circulating H5N1 clade, but cross-reactivity against heterologous clades is inconsistent and mostly inferior. Recombinant hemagglutinin vaccines containing conserved stalk epitopes show slight enhancement of cross-clade responses, yet neutralizing titers against divergent H5N1 subclades often fall below presumptive protective thresholds without booster immunizations. Discover more vaccine Find Vaccines vaccines Get Cold Relief Read Med Journals A crucial limitation the review emphasizes is the near-total absence of real-world effectiveness data. Because there has been no sustained human-to-human transmission of H5N1, H5N1 vaccines have generated few field effectiveness data, and licensure and stockpiling decisions have instead relied on surrogate endpoints, namely immunogenicity correlates such as HAI and neutralization titers. Pre-licensure clinical experience in high-risk groups such as poultry workers shows acceptable safety and immunogenicity, but conclusive protective effectiveness against natural H5N1 infection has not been determined. The authors conclude that although these vaccines achieve high immunogenicity against homologous strains when boosted with adjuvant, their heterologous breadth is poor, and no real-world effectiveness data currently exist to support vaccine optimization and real-time assessment during an actual outbreak. The review then turns to next-generation candidates designed for safety, immunogenicity and cross-protection. It describes H5N1 as now an established, year-round infection whose host range has expanded to include many mammals. The virus’s pathogenicity is attributed mainly to the acquisition of a polybasic hemagglutinin cleavage site, which facilitates systemic spread, alongside emerging endothelial and neurotropic properties that produce severe disease and high mortality in mammals. H5N1 has been observed to accumulate mutations linked to mammalian adaptation, albeit with limited transmission, notably in the hemagglutinin and polymerase complex. Novel mammalian reservoirs, including dairy cattle in the United States, raise human exposure risk. In response, vaccine development is shifting from older adjuvanted inactivated vaccines toward new platforms, with mRNA and virus-like particle vaccines being developed for rapid rollout and broader immune responses. The review highlights the speed of mRNA production, achievable in weeks from sequence reads, as making it one of the most promising pandemic platforms. Discover more Study Biology Track Outbreaks Biology textbook sales Several experimental strategies receive detailed attention. Live attenuated influenza virus vaccines based on NS segment truncation prevent influenza virus infection and have shown promise as vaccine candidates among other platforms, though the authors argue that more attention must turn to LAIV development platforms to produce a truly universal influenza vaccine candidate. H5 antigens expressed by recombinant adenovirus and vesicular stomatitis virus vectors induce robust humoral and cellular immunity in individuals after single vaccinations; non-replicating adenoviral vectors have proven safe, while replicating VSV platforms can activate stronger innate responses. Pre-existing anti-vector immunity is a recognized consideration, which heterologous prime-boost regimens can alleviate. Structure-guided design uses computational algorithms to stabilize hemagglutinin in its pre-fusion conformation or to display conserved epitopes, and computationally optimized broadly reactive antigens, known as COBRAs, extend cross-clade neutralization by concentrating on conserved regions. These antigens can be deployed across delivery systems including mRNA and protein formats. Universal strategies targeting the conserved hemagglutinin stem, the matrix protein 2 ectodomain, or internal proteins such as NP and M1 aim to induce cross-subtype protection, while chimeric hemagglutinin constructs, mosaic nanoparticles and multi-epitope chimeras are designed to address the antigenic diversity of H5 lineages. Despite these efforts, the review notes a persistent lack of broadly reactive antibody induction capable of conferring broad, long-lasting protection. The review also examines what determines whether an H5N1 vaccine works in a given person or population. AS03-adjuvanted H5N1 vaccine generates substantially more potent immune responses than non-adjuvanted vaccine, and one study found the adjuvant may boost responses by switching off the NR4A1 and SDC1 genes, switching on the ID3 gene and decreasing cortisol production, although the mechanisms remain incompletely understood. Two-dose schedules with a three-week interval have induced high neutralizing antibody levels against the vaccine strain, and protection may persist for up to three years after two doses of AS03-adjuvanted H5N1 vaccines, with the prime-boost schedule producing robust anamnestic antibody responses that are cross-protective. However, prior antigenic imprinting, or original antigenic sin, can shape future responses: B cell repertoires focus on conserved epitopes from initial exposure, which may suppress responses to new antigenic variants and limit the breadth of neutralizing responses, potentially reducing seroconversion to drifted antigens. Host genetics also matter, as single-nucleotide polymorphisms in pattern recognition receptors such as TLR7 and TLR8 and in cytokine regulators determine the magnitude of antibody and T cell responses. Immunosenescence reduces both humoral and cellular responses, with most older adults showing reduced HAI titers after vaccination, and comorbidities such as diabetes, cardiovascular disease and immunosuppression further lower responsiveness. On the viral side, antigenic drift in hemagglutinin and neuraminidase can produce circulating strains that no longer match vaccine antigens, reassortment can generate combinations against which pre-existing immunity offers minimal protection, and suboptimal poultry vaccination coverage can select drifted variants in reservoirs, complicating human vaccine strain selection and raising zoonotic spillover risk. Adjuvants emerge as a key lever for improving and shaping immunity. Oil-in-water adjuvants such as AS03 and MF59 enable reduced antigen doses while achieving high seroconversion by improving antigen uptake by antigen-presenting cells and triggering strong innate signaling, a dose-sparing capability the review considers especially valuable when pandemic production capacity is limited. Adjuvants activate diverse B cell clones and drive antibody affinity maturation, which may expand cross-clade neutralization breadth against antigenically drifted H5N1 strains, an important property for prepandemic stockpiles where the future strain cannot be predicted. Newer adjuvant preparations containing toll-like receptor agonists and synthetic immunomodulators are engineered to favor balanced Th1 and Th2 responses. In older adults and immunocompromised individuals, who commonly respond poorly to unadjuvanted vaccines, higher doses of adjuvants that strongly activate innate immunity, such as TLR4 agonists and saponin formulations, can partially address these limitations by enhancing dendritic cell activation and cytokine milieus. The review cautions that reactogenicity, including local pain and temporary systemic responses, must be assessed, and that while AS03 and MF59 have shown generally acceptable safety in controlled studies, continued surveillance across different populations is crucial. On evaluation methods, the review describes a tiered pathway. Preclinical tests in mice, ferrets and non-human primates establish safety, immunogenicity and protective effect, with ferrets considered the best influenza model because their respiratory anatomy and disease progression resemble those of humans. Hemagglutination inhibition titers remain a key correlate of protection, neutralization tests measure the strength of serum antibodies against viral entry, and cellular immunity assays such as ELISpot and intracellular cytokine staining quantify T cell responses associated with viral clearance and cross-clade coverage. Robust correlates accelerate development by reducing reliance on large efficacy trials that are ethically or logistically infeasible in prepandemic phases. Phase III randomized controlled trials in target populations typically use laboratory-confirmed influenza illness as the endpoint, and after approval, observational studies including cohort, case-control and test-negative designs assess real-world performance against circulating strains. Recent work illustrates the pipeline: an mRNA vaccine expressing the hemagglutinin of the first bovine-to-human strain, A/Texas/37/2024, stimulated strong, long-lasting humoral responses and a balanced Th1/Th2 profile in murine studies, providing 100 percent protection against lethal homologous challenge, and produced cross-clade binding antibody responses with a favorable safety profile in rats. Separately, an H5N1 vaccine cultured in serum-free MDCK cell suspension protected mice in challenge tests, with antibodies persisting up to eight months after the first dose in rats and elevated IP-10 levels indicating enhanced innate immunity. The review closes by linking vaccine science to delivery and equity. Intradermal delivery targets the immune cells of the skin and can achieve strong responses with lower antigen doses, extending scarce vaccine supplies, though it requires specialized equipment and trained personnel. Microneedle patches offer needle-free, self-administered vaccination with thermostability that removes cold-chain constraints, although mass production and regulatory licensing remain hurdles. Mucosal intranasal vaccines induce both systemic and mucosal immunity at the respiratory entry point but face stability and formulation issues. Heterologous prime-boost regimens, such as intramuscular priming followed by mucosal boosting, may make immunity more durable and broader against variants. The authors argue that equitable global distribution frameworks such as COVAX must ensure vaccines reach low- and middle-income countries based on need, with high-risk groups prioritized. Combined, they conclude, universal vaccines targeting conserved regions, durable cross-protection, agile manufacturing and fair distribution form the strategy they identify as most capable of reducing the threat of an H5N1 pandemic and improving global preparedness. Subject of Research: Universal influenza vaccine platforms for H5N1 pandemic preparedness Article Title: Recent advances in universal influenza vaccine platforms: paving the way for universal H5N1 vaccines and pandemic readiness Article References: Ali, S., Bibi, H., Ullah, H., Mehmood, M. S., Kamal, M. A., & Nazari, M. J. (2026). Recent advances in universal influenza vaccine platforms: paving the way for universal H5N1 vaccines and pandemic readiness.
