B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

实验性 mRNA 疫苗 保护泌乳奶牛免受 H5N1 感染 - Bioengineer.org

Experimental mRNA vaccine shields lactating dairy cows from H5N1 infection - Bioengineer.org
新闻简报针对 H5N1 流感病毒的实验性 mRNA-脂质纳米颗粒 疫苗 在哺乳奶牛中进行了测试,显示出对感染的保护作用,并将牛奶中的病毒 RNA 水平显着降低了约 1,000 倍。该研究于 2026 年 10 月 28 日发表,涉及一种自 2024 年以来在美国奶牛群中传播的 H5N1 病毒株。疫苗 耐受性良好,没有不良反应报道,并在血清和牛奶中引发了强烈的抗体反应。临床结果表明,与未接种疫苗的奶牛相比,接种疫苗的奶牛经历的乳腺炎和乳腺纤维化较轻。虽然 疫苗 减少了疾病和脱落,但它并没有消除感染,作者强调需要进一步的现场实验来评估其对 传播 的影响。该研究估计每头受影响奶牛的潜在经济损失为 950 美元,研究牛群的经济损失总计为 737,500 美元,强调了控制奶牛中的 H5N1 病毒以防止 跨物种溢出 感染人类和其他动物的重要性。
查看新闻五要素
时间
该研究于 2026 年 10 月 28 日发表,实验结果截至该日报告。挑战研究是在接种后几天内进行的。
地点与对象
该研究涉及美国的泌乳奶牛,特别关注自 2024 年以来在奶牛群中传播的 H5N1 病毒株。
事件
针对 H5N1 病毒的实验性 mRNA-脂质纳米颗粒 疫苗 在哺乳期奶牛中进行了测试,显示出对感染的保护作用,并将牛奶中的病毒 RNA 减少了约 1,000 倍。疫苗 耐受性良好,没有不良反应报告。
规模、影响与风险
与未接种疫苗的奶牛相比,疫苗 显着降低了接种疫苗的奶牛牛奶中的病毒 RNA 水平、乳腺炎和乳腺纤维化。它不能消除感染,但病毒脱落的减少可能会限制 传播。该研究估计,每头受影响的奶牛的潜在经济损失为 950 美元,所研究牛群的经济损失总计为 737,500 美元。
应对、状态与不确定性
该研究是一项对照挑战研究,疫苗 的有效性通过病毒 RNA 测量、抗体反应和临床结果得到证实。然而,还需要进一步大规模的现场实验来评估其对动物间 传播 的影响。作者还建议优化 疫苗接种 时间表和剂量以获得更好的保护。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Experimental mRNA vaccine shields lactating dairy cows from H5N1 infection - Bioengineer.org

News Brief
An experimental mRNA–lipid nanoparticle vaccine against H5N1 influenza virus was tested in lactating dairy cows, showing protection from infection and significantly reducing viral RNA levels in milk by about 1,000-fold. The study, published on October 28, 2026, involved a strain of H5N1 that has spread through U.S. dairy herds since 2024. The vaccine was well tolerated, with no adverse effects reported, and elicited robust antibody responses in both serum and milk. Clinical outcomes indicated that vaccinated cows experienced less severe mastitis and mammary fibrosis compared to unvaccinated ones. While the vaccine reduced disease and shedding, it did not sterilize infection, and the authors emphasized the need for further field experiments to assess its impact on transmission. The study estimated potential economic losses of $950 per affected cow, totaling $737,500 for the herd studied, highlighting the importance of controlling H5N1 in dairy cattle to prevent spillovers into humans and other animals.
Original source text (English)
HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER Friday, October 30, 2026 No Result View All Result Login HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER HOME NEWS EXPLORE CAREER Companies Jobs Lecturer PhD Studentship Postdoc Research Assistant EVENTS iGEM News Team PHOTOS VIDEO WIKI BLOG COMMUNITY FACEBOOK INSTAGRAM TWITTER No Result View All Result No Result View All Result Home NEWS Science News Biology Experimental mRNA vaccine shields lactating dairy cows from H5N1 infection by October 28, 2026 in Biology Reading Time: 4 mins read 0 Experimental mRNA vaccine shields lactating dairy cows from H5N1 infection Share on FacebookShare on TwitterShare on LinkedinShare on RedditShare on Telegram An experimental mRNA–lipid nanoparticle vaccine against highly pathogenic avian influenza H5N1 protected lactating dairy cows from infection in a small controlled challenge study, cutting peak viral RNA in milk by roughly 1,000-fold and sharply reducing the mastitis and mammary fibrosis that the virus causes in infected udders. The vaccine, described in Nature Biotechnology, encodes the hemagglutinin of A/dairy cattle/Texas/24-008749-002/2024, a strain representative of the clade 2.3.4.4b H5N1 viruses that have spread through dairy herds in the United States since 2024, causing mastitis and steep drops in milk production. The vaccine was well tolerated, with no obvious adverse effects reported. Because previous studies had detected trace amounts of vaccine-related mRNA in human milk after SARS-CoV-2 mRNA vaccination, the researchers also tested whether vaccine-derived mRNA could be found in bovine milk. None of the samples from vaccinated animals contained detectable H5 mRNA, indicating that vaccine-derived mRNA was not present at measurable levels in milk after intramuscular administration. Immunologically, the vaccine performed as designed. H5-reactive IgG appeared in serum as early as 21 days after the first dose and rose significantly after the booster, and functional assays confirmed that serum antibodies could neutralize the challenge virus. Hemagglutination inhibition assays showed that antibodies from all four vaccinated animals blocked viral attachment to red blood cells before challenge. H5-reactive IgG was also detected in milk, appearing in only one vaccinated cow after the prime but in all four after the boost, although milk antibody levels were lower than serum levels and declined more rapidly. The researchers could not run neutralization tests on milk because nonspecific inhibitors in milk from unvaccinated animals interfered with the assay. Discover more vaccine Find Vaccines dairy cattle Manage Livestock dairy cows Deep mutational scanning of the viral hemagglutinin revealed that the neutralizing antibodies in vaccinated cow sera targeted antigenic sites A and B on the globular head of the HA protein, near the receptor-binding domain. The amino acid identities at these strongly targeted sites are not highly variable among circulating clade 2.3.4.4b viruses, suggesting the vaccine is well matched to the strains currently infecting dairy cattle. The authors noted that continued surveillance will be needed to detect any future antigenic changes at these sites. After challenge, the differences between the groups were stark. In unvaccinated cows, H5N1 viral RNA in milk from inoculated mammary quarters was first detected one day after inoculation, peaked at two days, and remained detectable throughout the study period. In vaccinated cows, viral RNA levels were significantly lower at multiple time points, with peak titers about 1,000-fold lower than in unvaccinated animals. Most milk samples that tested positive by PCR with a cycle threshold below 35 contained infectious virus, underscoring the relevance of the RNA measurements. Notably, the researchers found evidence that virus migrated from inoculated to uninoculated mammary quarters in two unvaccinated cows, with viral RNA, infectious virus and viral antigen detected in milk and tissue from uninoculated quarters, whereas viral RNA in milk from uninoculated quarters of vaccinated animals remained below the limit of detection. Discover more infectious infecting infection Clinical outcomes tracked the virological results. Unvaccinated cows showed decreased rumen motility and significant drops in milk production beginning two days after inoculation, with a dramatic decline at day three that persisted through much of the monitoring period. Their milk showed severe changes in color and consistency indicative of mastitis, and California mastitis test scores in inoculated quarters consistently indicated moderate to severe inflammation. Vaccinated cows were far less affected on all these measures, and no significant differences in rectal temperature were observed between the groups. At necropsy, inoculated mammary quarters from unvaccinated cows had macroscopic lesion scores of 95.4 percent compared with 8.1 percent in vaccinated cows, and microscopic scoring of collagen staining showed significantly less mammary fibrosis in the vaccinated group. The authors emphasize that the vaccine reduced disease and shedding but did not sterilize infection. While it is reasonable to suspect that the large reduction in viral titers in milk would limit animal-to-animal transmission, they write that additional large-scale field experiments will be required to test that possibility directly. They also note that future work should optimize vaccination schedules and doses, explore whether a single immunization could elicit protective antibody levels in milk, and consider a differentiating infected from vaccinated animals strategy, since the vaccine elicits antibodies only to the hemagglutinin and detection of nucleoprotein antibodies could indicate natural infection. Discover more Get Fresh Dairy Read Med Journals Track Biotech Check Symptoms Study Biology The economic stakes are considerable. The authors cite a study estimating losses of 950 US dollars per clinically affected cow in an infected herd, totaling approximately 737,500 dollars for the herd studied, driven by decreased milk production, mortality and early herd removal. Because the main site of H5N1 replication in cattle is mammary tissue rather than the respiratory tract, and transmission between animals is thought to occur through viral shedding in milk, a vaccine that reduces virus in milk could be particularly valuable for controlling spread within and between farms, while also limiting potential spillovers into humans and other susceptible animals. The authors also note that they did not assess T cell-mediated immunity in this study, although their earlier work in calves found that mRNA–LNP vaccination induces potent CD8+ T cell responses. They conclude that the H5 mRNA–LNP vaccine is safe and immunogenic in lactating dairy cows, eliciting robust antibody responses in serum and milk while reducing clinical disease, virus shedding in milk and mammary gland fibrosis, and they position it as an additional tool to be combined with existing control measures and robust viral testing programs rather than a standalone solution. The vaccine significantly reduced viral RNA levels in milk, mastitis, and mammary fibrosis in vaccinated cows compared to unvaccinated ones. The study was a controlled challenge study, and the vaccine's effectiveness was confirmed through viral RNA measurements, antibody responses, and clinical outcomes. The authors also suggest optimizing vaccination schedules and doses for better protection.

禽流感威胁着世界秃鹫——其后果可能会蔓延

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu threatens the world’s vultures – and the consequences could spread

News Brief
A deadly strain of H5N1 avian influenza is threatening vulture populations worldwide, causing significant mortality in species like California condors, Griffon vultures, and bearded vultures. The virus spreads through carcasses, creating a dangerous feedback loop that could further decimate vulture populations. Conservationists are advised to monitor populations, implement vaccination programs, manage feeding stations, follow strict biosecurity protocols, and develop context-specific national action plans.
Original source text (English)
Subscribe news videos images take action earthpedia earthsnap Subscribe Griffon vultures are among the scavenging birds threatened by H5N1 avian influenza. Credit: Shutterstock October 28, 2026 Bird flu threatens the world’s vultures – and the consequences could spread A deadly strain of bird flu is threatening vulnerable vulture populations worldwide, raising concerns about disease spread and ecosystem health. ByAndrei Ionescu Staff Writer Follow Earth on Google Vultures do something nobody else will. They eat what is already dead, often rotten, often crawling with pathogens that would kill most other animals. Their remarkable ability to survive exposure to many dangerous pathogens makes them indispensable to healthy ecosystems. Without vultures, carcasses accumulate, bacteria spread, disease moves up the food chain. They are, in a real sense, the biological sanitation system of the terrestrial world. Now the world’s vultures are facing something their natural defenses cannot reliably protect them against. A recently published letter warns that the H5N1 avian influenza virus poses a catastrophic threat to vulture populations already thinned by poisoning, habitat loss, and persecution. Vultures were already in trouble At least 16 of the world’s 23 vulture species are already of conservation concern. The California condor has fewer than 600 individuals alive, including those in captivity. The Andean condor ranges across a continent but in decreasing numbers. Griffon vultures in southern Europe were considered a conservation success story. None of this provides any buffer against H5N1. The scale of what has already happened is sobering. Thousands of American black vultures and turkey vultures have died across the United States and South America. In southern Europe, the 2022 H5N1 outbreak killed at least 16 adult Griffon vultures across Spain and France and left hundreds of chicks dead at breeding sites. California condors, whose total population had fallen to just 27 birds in 1987, lost roughly 6 percent of their wild population in just six weeks. Bearded vultures in Europe have been affected too. Documentation from Africa and Asia remains sparse, partly because surveillance is limited and partly because vulture numbers there have already declined so severely that detecting further losses is harder. A dangerous feedback loop The letter identifies a feedback loop that makes the situation especially dangerous. Vultures are social and congregate at carcasses – the same places where H5N1-infected animals are most likely to have died. As scavengers, they are resistant to many bacterial pathogens, but H5N1 is different. The virus kills with high efficiency across domestic and wild birds alike. It has now been confirmed in over 48 mammal species worldwide. The loop works like this. More infected animals die in the landscape, producing more contaminated carcasses. Vultures gather at these carcasses and contract the virus. As more vultures die, there are fewer birds to remove the carrion. “The greater the number of infected carcasses, the greater the risk of contagion for the vultures,” Morant said. “Since these carcasses can become sources of infection, a paradox arises: as more vultures die, the capacity to eliminate the carrion reduces, the pathogens spread and contagion increases.” What makes this particularly alarming is that many vulture species are already living close to the edge. A virus that kills 6 percent of a California condor population in six weeks is not a manageable mortality event for a species with a few hundred wild individuals. Such losses could undo decades of conservation progress. Tracking outbreaks before they spread The letter proposes five coordinated responses. These are framed explicitly as a guide for conservation managers and policymakers across the diverse range of countries where vultures survive. The first and most urgent is population monitoring. Without knowing where birds are dying and at what rate, it is impossible to calibrate any other response. Migratory species – which cross national borders and carry the virus with them – need particular attention. “In the Basque Country, the Griffon vulture population is in a healthy condition, but the Egyptian vulture is more sensitive. We need to pay particular attention to migratory species,” Morant said. The second is vaccination, where logistical conditions allow. In response to the acute threat to California condors, researchers have already implemented a vaccination program. The letter advocates continuous evaluation of vaccination as populations come under serious risk. Third, feeding stations – a conservation tool widely used for vultures across Europe and parts of Africa – need to be managed so that the carcasses provided are verified free of H5N1. These stations are, by design, places where large numbers of birds aggregate. In the context of a highly contagious pathogen, that aggregation becomes a liability unless the food supply is controlled. Biosecurity protocols and context-specific actions Fourth, researchers working with vultures must follow strict biosecurity protocols since the virus can persist in feathers and tissue after death. Fifth, the letter calls for context-specific national action plans embedded in shared cross-border governance frameworks. The countries that host significant vulture populations range across vastly different socioeconomic contexts and regulatory environments. A one-size approach will not work. But the absence of coordination across borders will not work either, given that the birds themselves do not recognize frontiers. Why vultures cannot recover quickly Vulture populations once collapsed can take decades to recover, if they recover at all. The birds are long-lived and slow to reproduce – most species raise only one chick per year. A sustained mortality event can hollow out a population without any dramatic visible collapse, simply by preventing the replacement of birds that die. H5N1 adds rapid, acute mortality on top of that slow demographic pressure. “We wanted to alert the research community and those responsible for conservation so that they could establish coordinated actions,” Morant said. “To tackle this dangerous epidemiological situation, it is urgent to develop coordinated cross-border plans and to integrate these actions into shared governance structures.” The birds that clean up after everything else now need someone to look after them. The study is published in the journal Conservation Biology. — Like what you read? Subscribe to our newsletter for engaging articles, exclusive content, and the latest updates. Location and population

Article Open access 高致病性禽流感(HPAI)clade 2.3.4.4b H5N1病毒近期在美国泌乳奶牛中暴发,主要引起乳腺炎与产奶量骤降。该病毒主要复制场所为乳腺组织而非呼吸道,动物间传播被认为通过乳汁中病毒排出实现。研究人员构建了一种编码A/dairy cattle/Texas/24-008749-002/2024株血凝素(HA)的mRNA–脂质纳米颗粒(mRNA–LNP)疫苗,并在泌乳奶牛中评估其免疫原性与保护效力。实验将8头奶牛分为疫苗组与对照组,分别接受两剂肌肉注射疫苗或生理盐水,随后进行乳腺内病毒攻毒。接种可在血清中诱导体液免疫,产生HA反应性抗体与中和抗体,并在乳汁中检出HA反应性抗体。攻毒后,疫苗组乳汁中病毒RNA峰值降低约1000倍,乳腺炎与乳腺纤维化发生率显著下降。结果表明,该mRNA–LNP疫苗可高效抑制乳腺内H5N1复制并减轻临床损伤,为奶牛禽流感防控提供新策略。 论文解读:H5N1 clade 2.3.4.4b mRNA–LNP疫苗保护泌乳奶牛抵御乳腺内感染的研究进展 点击免费下载【综合性的体外药效评价解决方案】,全方位支持您的抗体药研发项目,快速地完成体外药效试验以及获得优质药效的先导分子。领 取 研究背景与立项依据 高致病性禽流感(HPAI,highly pathogenic avian influenza)clade 2.3.4.4b H5N1病毒自2024年起在美国奶牛群中持续传播,造成泌乳奶牛严重乳腺炎、产奶量断崖式下降及重大经济损失。与典型流感不同,该病毒不主要侵袭呼吸道,而是特异嗜性乳腺分泌上皮,病毒经乳汁排出成为牛群内传播核心环节;同时跨种传播至人群的风险引发公共卫生关切。现有防控依赖生物安全与管理,缺乏针对泌乳奶牛、面向乳腺局部保护的高效疫苗。因此,研究人员拟验证一种编码流行株HA的核苷修饰mRNA–脂质纳米颗粒(mRNA–LNP)疫苗能否在奶牛体内诱导系统性与泌乳腺局部免疫,并阻断乳腺内感染与病毒脱落。 主要技术方法概述 研究纳入宾夕法尼亚州HPAI监测阴性荷斯坦泌乳奶牛8头(2–8岁),分疫苗组与盐水对照组各4头,采用prime–boost肌注免疫。关键方法包括:定量PCR(qPCR)检测乳中疫苗mRNA残留;酶联免疫吸附试验(ELISA)测血清与乳中HA特异性IgG;焦点减少中和试验(FRNT)与血凝抑制试验(HAI)评估功能抗体;基于假病毒深度突变扫描(deep mutational scanning)定位血清中和表位;乳腺内接种同源H5N1病毒攻毒;RT–qPCR监测多样本病毒RNA;加州乳腺炎试验(CMT)与组织学天狼星红染色评估宏观/微观纤维化;免疫组化(IHC)检测病毒核蛋白(NP)抗原。 研究结果 H5 mRNA–LNP疫苗在泌乳奶牛中安全性良好 研究人员在首免与加强后连续采集乳汁,以高灵敏qPCR检测疫苗来源H5 mRNA,下限为65拷贝/μl,结果所有乳样均未检出疫苗mRNA,证明肌注后疫苗核酸不向乳汁可检测性转移,对乳品安全与奶牛本身均无直接残留风险。 H5 mRNA–LNP疫苗在泌乳奶牛中具有强免疫原性 接种后21天即可见HA特异性IgG上升,加强后显著升高;FRNT显示血清具备高效中和能力,HAI证实抗体可阻断红细胞凝集。乳汁中加强后亦出现HA反应性IgG,但滴度低于血清且衰减更快。少数动物入组前存在低水平交叉反应IgG,归因于人流感H1暴露,不影响疫苗应答。 疫苗诱导抗体的表位特异性 借助假病毒深度突变扫描,研究人员发现中和抗体集中识别HA球状头区抗原位点A与B(邻近受体结合域),这些位点在circulating clade 2.3.4.4b病毒中高度保守,提示该疫苗诱导的免疫压力不易被当前流行株逃逸。 H5 mRNA–LNP接种保护泌乳奶牛抵御H5N1感染 乳腺内攻毒后,对照组乳中病毒RNA于第2天达峰并持续阳性;疫苗组接种乳区病毒RNA峰值降低约1000倍,多时间点显著更低,且未接种乳区病毒扩散受限。鼻眼拭子、血液等样本中疫苗组检出极少。产奶量方面,对照组自攻毒后第2–3天骤降且长期不能恢复,疫苗组下降轻微;反刍活动亦受保护。乳腺炎评分(颜色、黏稠度、CMT)显示疫苗组几乎无中重度炎症。 疫苗显著降低乳腺纤维化 剖检与组织学分析表明,对照组接种乳区宏观病变评分95.4% vs 疫苗组8.1%;天狼星红染色显示疫苗组小叶内与叶间胶原沉积明显减少,微观纤维化评分更低,有效遏制坏死性乳腺炎后的纤维替代。 讨论与结论翻译 讨论指出,clade 2.3.4.4b在牛群的经济损失每名临床牛约950美元,疫苗联合检测可压低传播与跨种风险。本研究系统量化了mRNA–LNP对乳腺内感染的防护:不仅降病毒载量,还保产奶、减纤维化。乳汁抗体虽低于血清,但局部中和潜力存在;未来需优化免疫程序、单剂持久性、DIVA(区分感染与接种动物)策略。表位保守性支持该平台匹配当前流行株,且既往研究提示可辅以T细胞免疫防逃逸。 研究结论部分译述如下: 综上,H5 mRNA–LNP 2.3.4.4b疫苗在泌乳奶牛中安全、免疫原性明确,可于血清与乳汁诱导强健HA抗体;攻毒后显著削弱临床疾病(尤其产奶损失),大幅降低乳中病毒脱落与乳腺纤维化。该疫苗可作为现行管控措施之外的补充工具,用于抑制农场内暴发、降低场间传播并缓解乳业经济损害。 你想把这篇解读压缩成投稿用的“审稿意见摘要”,还是会议汇报用的“研究亮点”? 审稿摘要 研究亮点 审稿摘要 研究亮点 打赏 Utah Has No Plan to Cull Bird Flu Mink as Genomes Reveal a Mutation Also Found in Spain's Fur Farm Outbreak - Nature World News

News Brief
The article from Nature World News, published on 2026-10-27, discusses the lack of a culling plan for mink infected with bird flu in Utah. It highlights that genomes from these mink show a mutation also found in a recent outbreak at Spain's fur farms. The report does not provide specific details on the scale of the impact or risk associated with the mutation. The focus is on the genetic findings and the absence of a targeted response in Utah. The article is part of a broader collection of news on environmental and health-related topics, including other animal diseases and climate-related events. The evidence suggests that the mutation is a point of concern for potential zoonotic transmission and the spread of avian influenza, but no confirmed cases or detailed response measures are mentioned.

专家警告称,特朗普政府失去了对禽流感的控制 - 雅虎

Trump administration loses grip on bird flu, experts warn - Yahoo
新闻简报特朗普政府因未能控制在美国传播于貂和牛场的禽流感暴发而受到批评。该病毒H5N1已感染美国数十个州的700多群牲畜,造成重大经济损失。尽管自1990年代末以来已感染约1000人,死亡率约为50%死亡率,但病毒突变的风险依然很高。州官员优先考虑经济利益而非公共卫生,联邦遏制病毒的努力也有所削弱。
查看新闻五要素
时间
特朗普政府因失去对在美国各地水貂和养牛场传播的禽流感 暴发 的控制而受到批评。
地点与对象
美国,包括犹他州、西班牙、芬兰和华盛顿。
事件
特朗普政府因失去对在水貂和养牛场中传播的禽流感 暴发 的控制而受到批评,该病毒目前在美国养殖的奶牛中流行,并感染了数十个州的 700 多个牛群。暴发 正在接受调查,但尚未确认为大流行威胁。
规模、影响与风险
自1990年代末以来,该病毒已感染约1000人,死亡率约为50%死亡率。每例人类感染都会增加病毒突变的风险。该病毒暴发已导致野生动物和家畜家禽造成毁灭性损失,美国自2024年以来已有超过72人感染H5N1,主要通过接触患病奶牛而传播。
应对、状态与不确定性
专家表示,美国正在允许病毒肆虐,政府官员将经济利益置于公共卫生之上。联邦官员未能进行干预,要求疾病预防控制中心和美国农业部大幅削减开支的呼声削弱了遏制病毒的努力。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Trump administration loses grip on bird flu, experts warn - Yahoo

News Brief
The Trump administration is criticized for losing control of a bird flu outbreak that has spread among mink and cattle farms in the United States. The virus, H5N1, has infected over 700 herds across dozens of states and caused significant economic losses. While it has infected around 1,000 people since the late 1990s with a 50% mortality rate, the risk of mutation remains high. State officials are prioritizing financial interests over public health, and federal efforts to contain the virus have been weakened.
Original source text (English)
The Trump administration has been criticised for losing control of a bird flu outbreak that has spread among mink and cattle farms across the United States. The H5N1 virus has infected around 1,000 people since it was first detected in the late 1990s, killing roughly half of them. While it currently does not spread among humans, scientists have long worried that it could trigger a pandemic, and that each human infection raises the risk of the virus mutating. A new variant emerged in 2020 and has since spread to millions of birds and hundreds of animal species across the globe, leading to devastating losses in both wildlife and farmed livestock and poultry. While some countries have introduced tough measures to curb its spread, including mandatory culls of infected animals, "flockdowns" (where birds are kept indoors) and vaccination of farm workers who handle at-risk livestock, experts say the US is allowing the virus to run rampant. For more than two months, an outbreak has raged unchecked on a Utah farm that raises thousands of mink for fur, news outlet ProPublica reported last week. Mink in captivity have previously been described as pandemic "time bombs". The size and density of the farms give invading viruses a perfect environment in which to mutate . Mink have very similar respiratory systems to humans, increasing the risk that a virus can spread between the animals and people, and undergo a process known as "reassortment", when two or more viruses mix together to make a new, more dangerous strain. H5N1 is now endemic in US farmed cows - AFP via Getty Images/RONALDO SCHEMIDT Despite calls for the animals to be culled, state officials have refused to mandate any action, ProPublica revealed. Reports suggest Utah state officials are prioritising the financial interests of the fur trade, most of which is exported to China and Russia, and that federal officials have failed to intervene. Outbreaks of H5N1 in farmed mink in Spain and Finland have resulted in mass culls, and several countries, including Britain and, just last week, Denmark, historically the largest producer of mink fur in the world, have banned mink farming over its disease risks. "I can't completely wrap my head around it. And you still have workers going into the mink farm, and it's flu season, so this is when there's a chance for new recombinants to occur," Marc Johnson, a virologist at the University of Missouri, told The Telegraph. It's not just the mink outbreak that is concerning scientists. H5N1 'jumped' from birds into dairy cattle in late 2023 and is now endemic in US farmed cows. Experts say the outbreak is not only costing the dairy industry millions of dollars, but also raises the risk that the virus could mutate to spread more easily between mammals. More than 700 herds across dozens of states have now been infected with H5N1. While it rarely causes death in cattle, it can cause severe illness and lead to lower milk yield. Each new infection also raises the risk to humans. At least 72 people have caught H5N1 in the US since 2024, mostly through working with sick dairy cattle. Last week the US recorded its first case of H5N1 this year in a farm worker from Washington. Experts say deep cuts to the US Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC), the agency responsible for managing human health threats, and to the US Department of Agriculture (USDA) have weakened efforts to contain it. "A year ago, we heard that the federal workers responsible for controlling the dairy outbreak had been fired," said Dr Jennifer Nuzzo, professor of epidemiology and director of the pandemic centre at Brown University School of Public Health, whose team originally helped manage the outbreak. "We haven't heard much since then about what's going on, nor what has been restored, or whether testing programmes are ongoing." Federal officials and disease experts like Dr Nuzzo used to hold frequent calls. "The calls were an opportunity for us to say, 'Have you considered this approach?' and to ask why they were taking certain actions," she said. The calls no longer happen, which she called "a great loss". "The virus is still in birds, but the cow [outbreak] is completely preventable," said Dr Johnson. "Cattle infections last only a few weeks before the animals recover. If infected herds were simply isolated until the outbreak fizzled out, it would stop. "But cattle keep being moved around, spreading it into new states," said Dr Johnson, referring to the way the US dairy industry operates, in which cows are routinely transported across state lines, whether to join new herds, for breeding, or to replace animals at large milk-producing operations. Experts are calling on the government to introduce stricter biosecurity measures on farms, regulate the movement of cattle between states, and help to support better personal protection for farmworkers. Last year, the Trump administration also pulled the plug on a $766 million contract awarded to Moderna that would help to develop a human H5N1 vaccine, citing concerns over mRNA technology. "The cancellation [of the contract] means that the government is discarding what could be one of the most effective and rapid tools to combat an avian influenza outbreak," Amesh Adalja, senior scholar at the Johns Hopkins Center for Health, said at the time. The Trump administration has been criticised for losing control of a bird flu outbreak that has spread among mink and cattle farms across the United States.

United States, including Utah, Spain, Finland, and Washington.
Event
The Trump administration is criticized for losing control of a bird flu outbreak that has spread among mink and cattle farms, with the virus now endemic in US farmed cows and infecting over 700 herds across dozens of states. The outbreak is under investigation but not yet confirmed to be a pandemic threat.
Scale, impact and risk
The virus has infected around 1,000 people since the late 1990s, with 50% mortality. Each human infection raises the risk of the virus mutating. The outbreak has caused devastating losses in wildlife and farmed livestock and poultry, with over 72 people catching H5N1 in the US since 2024, mostly through working with sick dairy cattle.
Response, status and uncertainty
Experts say the US is allowing the virus to run rampant, with state officials prioritizing financial interests over public health. Federal officials have failed to intervene, and calls for deep cuts to the CDC and USDA have weakened efforts to contain the virus.

Bird flu outbreak at Utah mink farm sparks concerns about government response - The Guardian
新闻简报犹他州一家水貂养殖场爆发的禽流感 暴发 引发了人们对政府反应的担忧,特别是在特朗普政府的领导下。州兽医阿曼达·普莱斯 (Amanda Price) 试图向美国农业部寻求联邦援助,但被告知水貂养殖场不受监管,并且没有 疫苗 获得批准用于这些动物。虽然 暴发 并未导致人类确诊病例,但詹妮弗·努佐 (Jennifer Nuzzo) 等专家警告农场工人存在潜在风险,他们可能会从受感染的水貂身上感染病毒。州和联邦官员之间缺乏透明度和沟通,引发了人们对政府管理此类 暴发 准备情况的担忧。此外,美国农业部没有回应置评请求,农场主查德·芬恩 (Chad Fenn) 提到,这名工人的眼睛里有灰尘,但不建议进行测试。努佐还对特朗普政府期间对农场的监控比以前减少表示担忧,强调需要加强监督以防止潜在的流行病。

地点与对象
犹他州,美国;水貂养殖场工人。
事件
犹他州一家水貂养殖场发生的 暴发 禽流感疫情引起了人们对政府反应的担忧,该州兽医试图寻求联邦援助,但被告知美国农业部不监管水貂养殖场,并且缺乏针对该动物的批准的 疫苗。
规模、影响与风险
暴发 涉及盐湖城附近的一个水貂养殖场,没有确诊的人间病例。然而,由于水貂容易感染也会感染人类的​​病毒,人们担心可能会感染人类。据指出,接触患病动物的工人面临的风险最大。
应对、状态与不确定性
州兽医阿曼达·普莱斯向美国农业部寻求帮助,但没有得到支持。联邦疾病预防控制中心官员与犹他州卫生部门工作人员进行了交谈,但没有与普莱斯沟通。目前尚无确诊的人类感染病例,但缺乏透明度和对农场工人的监控引发了担忧。农场主查德·芬恩 (Chad Fenn) 表示,这名工人的眼睛里有灰尘,州卫生部门不建议进行检测。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu outbreak at Utah mink farm sparks concerns about government response - The Guardian

News Brief
A bird flu outbreak at a mink farm in Utah has raised concerns about the government's response, particularly under the Trump administration. The state veterinarian, Amanda Price, attempted to seek federal assistance from the USDA but was informed that mink farms are not regulated and no vaccine is approved for the animals. While the outbreak did not result in confirmed human cases, experts like Jennifer Nuzzo warned about the potential risk to farm workers, who could contract the virus from infected mink. The lack of transparency and communication between state and federal officials has sparked worries about the administration's preparedness for managing such outbreaks. Additionally, the USDA did not respond to a request for comment, and the farm owner, Chad Fenn, mentioned that the worker had dust in his eye, though no testing was recommended. Nuzzo also expressed concerns about reduced monitoring of farms during the Trump administration compared to previous ones, highlighting the need for better oversight to prevent potential pandemics.
Original source text (English)

Photograph: Alexandros Avramidis/Reuters View image in fullscreen A mink from an unrelated farm in Greece. Photograph: Alexandros Avramidis/Reuters Bird flu outbreak at Utah mink farm sparks concerns about government response State veterinarian attempted to get help from federal government but was told USDA didn’t regulate mink farms An outbreak of bird flu at a mink farm in Utah – and the response from its owner and regulators – has sparked concerns from infectious disease experts about the Trump administration’s ability to manage such viruses. While the mink becoming sick was unlikely to produce a “tidal wave of human infections”, an apparent lack of transparency from government officials about the infections was very troubling, said Jennifer Nuzzo, an epidemiology professor and director of the pandemic center at Brown University School of Public Health. “No animal or human will be protected from the toll of this virus through secrecy,” Nuzzo said in a phone interview on Saturday. “The risk is greatest among people who have exposure to sick animals. If that is not you, I think you can take this off your worry list, [but] it does not abdicate the government of its responsibility to try to prevent a pandemic.” ‘The silence was the worst part’: after culling his entire flock, US poultry farmer now fears bird flu Read more For two months, an outbreak of bird flu has spread at a farm about an hour from Salt Lake City that raises thousands of mink for their fur, ProPublica reported earlier this week. A state veterinarian, Amanda Price, handled the response and tried to get help from the US Department of Agriculture (USDA), but the federal officials told her that they didn’t regulate mink or have a vaccine approved for the animal, according to ProPublica, which obtained messages through the state’s public records law. The USDA did not immediately respond to a request for comment. Federal Centers for Disease Control and Prevention (CDC) officials spoke with Utah health department staff but did not communicate with Price, the outlet further reported. In other countries where such outbreaks have occurred, the government has ordered farms to kill their mink. Veterinarians told ProPublica that is what the Utah farm owners should already have done. This is not the first outbreak in recent times at a Utah fur farm. During the Covid-19 pandemic, nearly 10,000 mink in Utah died from the coronavirus and quarantines were implemented at nine farms. The virus was probably transmitted to the animals from infected workers, according to a report in the journal Emerging Infectious Diseases. Mink are very susceptible to infection with several viruses that also infect humans, according to a report from the journal Proceedings of the National Academy of Sciences of the United States of America. But Nuzzo said she didn’t “see any signals that the virus is spreading”. “The scenario I’m worried about is this one: workers who are exposed to these animals will get sick, and some of them might die, and that is awful because we potentially could prevent that through thinking about protecting farm workers with a vaccine, like other countries are using,” Nuzzo said. The virus could infect humans and become a pandemic if it were to combine with an influenza virus, “a worry that grows with the onset of each human flu season when there are more influenza viruses in circulation”, Nuzzo said. “I cannot tell you what the probability of that is. It could be zero,” she added. In Utah, a fur trade representative told Price that culling the mink at C&T Fenn Farms would kill the business and the industry, ProPublica reported. But Tom Peacock, an avian influenza researcher at the Pirbright Institute, told the news organization that in such situations, depopulation – ie, culling the mink – typically happens quickly and the fact that it didn’t in this case was “very concerning”. Officials reportedly also did not monitor the farm workers for symptoms, despite CDC guidance to do so. ProPublica found two people who worked at the farm, one of whom had a red eye. The federal health agency urges testing for that symptom. Price and the Utah department of agriculture and food did not immediately respond to a request for comment. The farm owner, Chad Fenn, told the news organization that the worker had dust in his eye and that the state health department did not recommend testing. The Guardian could not immediately reach Fenn. Nuzzo worries about how, she said, during Donald Trump’s second term, the federal government appears to be doing less monitoring of farms for such potential viruses than previous administrations. Shortly after Trump took office, his administration fired federal employees who were responding to a bird flu outbreak among dairy cows. Nuzzo said federal officials used to hold regular calls with disease experts such as her to “keep us informed about what they were doing”. That no longer happens, she said. “I found them quite useful because it allowed us to push them and say, ‘Well, what about this?’, and to interrogate some of the ideas,” Nuzzo said. “A lot is lost when that doesn’t happen ... It might mean that things are going on that people don’t see or know about. It might also be that actions don’t get the scrutiny they deserve.” Explore more on these topics Utah Bird flu Trump administration Infectious diseases Health features Share Reuse this content Most viewed Visit Norway makes shithouses ‘great again’ after Trump insult The low-budget Brewers won the World Series – and exposed baseball’s biggest lieHoward Bryant Patrick Stewart, Star Trek actor and acclaimed thespian, dies aged 86 Patrick Stewart: the legacy of domestic violence Live Trump again calls for prosecution of Mary Peltola as turmoil grows over Alaska campaign investigation – live Most viewed Most viewed

Location and population
Utah, United States; mink farm workers.
Event
An outbreak of bird flu at a mink farm in Utah raised concerns about the government's response, with the state veterinarian attempting to seek federal assistance but being told the USDA does not regulate mink farms and lacks approved vaccines for the animal.
Scale, impact and risk
The outbreak involved a mink farm near Salt Lake City, with no confirmed human cases. However, there were concerns about potential human infections due to mink's susceptibility to viruses that also infect humans. The risk was noted to be greatest among workers exposed to sick animals.

The state veterinarian, Amanda Price, sought help from the USDA but was not supported. Federal CDC officials spoke with Utah health department staff but did not communicate with Price. 里诺-斯帕克斯地区的鹅感染了禽流感 暴发,包括 UNR(更新) - 这里是里诺

Bird flu outbreak confirmed in geese across Reno-Sparks, including UNR (updated) - This Is Reno
新闻简报高致病性禽流感(俗称禽流感)的 暴发 已在里诺-斯帕克斯地区的多群鹅中得到证实,其中包括内华达大学里诺分校 (UNR) 校园的鹅群。内华达州野生动物部、内华达州农业部和内华达州公共和行为健康部正在共同努力管理这一情况。虽然公众感染的风险被认为很低,但官员建议避免直接接触生病或死亡的 野生鸟类,并让宠物和儿童远离他们。敦促家禽饲养者采取预防措施,例如保护家禽、对设备进行消毒以及监测疾病迹象。人类感染很少见,但在没有适当防护装备的情况下接触受感染的动物或受污染的表面可能会发生暴露。鼓励居民报告任何患病的鹅,并遵循指导方针,以防止病毒进一步传播。

在里诺-斯帕克斯地区的多群鹅中确认了 高致病性禽流感(禽流感)的 暴发,特别提到了内华达大学里诺校区的鹅。内华达州野生动物部、内华达州农业部和内华达州公共和行为健康部门正在协调对 暴发 的响应。

B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu outbreak confirmed in geese across Reno-Sparks, including UNR (updated) - This Is Reno

News Brief
An outbreak of highly pathogenic avian influenza, commonly known as bird flu, has been confirmed among multiple flocks of geese in the Reno-Sparks area, including those on the University of Nevada, Reno (UNR) campus. The Nevada Department of Wildlife, Nevada Department of Agriculture, and Nevada Division of Public and Behavioral Health are working together to manage the situation. While the risk of infection to the general public is considered low, officials advise avoiding direct contact with sick or dead wild birds and keeping pets and children away from them. Poultry owners are urged to take precautions such as securing their birds, disinfecting equipment, and monitoring for signs of illness. Human infections are rare, but exposure can occur through contact with infected animals or contaminated surfaces without proper protective gear. Residents are encouraged to report any sick geese and follow guidelines to prevent the virus from spreading further.
Original source text (English)
Skip to main content Sign in Home News Events Investigations Donate Advertise Subscribe 🇺🇲 Election 2026 Sign in Sign in Welcome!Log into your account your username your password Forgot your password? Privacy Policy Password recovery Recover your password your email Search 59.4 F Reno Home News Events Investigations Donate Advertise Subscribe More 🇺🇲 Election 2026 Sign in Try "researchers" Search Health Bird flu outbreak confirmed in geese across Reno-Sparks, including UNR (updated) October 29, 2026 Geese on the University of Nevada, Reno campus. Bob Conrad / THIS IS RENO By ThisIsReno Nevada wildlife and public health officials have confirmed an outbreak of highly pathogenic avian influenza, commonly known as bird flu, among multiple flocks of geese in the Reno-Sparks area. Geese at the University of Nevada, Reno were confirmed to be impacted. UNR President Brian Sandoval, in a campus email, said campus geese were confirmed sick. “The University is aware of sick and deceased geese observed near Manzanita Lake and is coordinating with NDOW,” Sandoval wrote. “The risk of infection to the general public is considered low, but people should avoid direct contact with sick or dead wild birds and keep pets and children away from them.” The Nevada Department of Wildlife, Nevada Department of Agriculture and Nevada Division of Public and Behavioral Health are coordinating their response to the outbreak. Officials are asking residents to report sick geese and take precautions when encountering dead birds to help prevent the virus from spreading. Highly pathogenic avian influenza is a contagious virus that affects wild birds and domestic poultry. It can also infect livestock and humans, although transmission to people is rare. LATEST VIDEOS nextstay CC SettingsOffArabicChineseEnglishFrenchGermanHindiPortugueseSpanish Font ColorwhiteFont Opacity100%Font Size100%Font FamilyArialText ShadownoneBackground ColorblackBackground Opacity50%Window ColorblackWindow Opacity0% WhiteBlackRedGreenBlueYellowMagentaCyan 100%75%50%25% 200%175%150%125%100%75%50% ArialGeorgiaGaramondCourier NewTahomaTimes New RomanTrebuchet MSVerdana NoneRaisedDepressedUniformDrop Shadow WhiteBlackRedGreenBlueYellowMagentaCyan 100%75%50%25%0% WhiteBlackRedGreenBlueYellowMagentaCyan 100%75%50%25%0% Video Muted FREE TO READ Most This Is Reno content is free. Get more Reno news in your inbox daily. It’s free. Unsubscribe anytime. Signs of infection in birds include coughing, sneezing, discharge from the eyes or nose, swelling around the eyes, poor balance, abnormal head or neck positioning, diarrhea, weakness and sudden death. Residents who encounter sick geese should leave the birds alone, keep children and pets away and report their location to the Nevada Department of Wildlife at 775-688-1500. People who find dead geese in public parks should contact the agency responsible for the property. Reno residents can reach city parks officials at 775-334-2270, while Washoe County Parks can be contacted at 775-828-6612. For dead waterfowl found on private property, officials recommend wearing gloves, an N95 mask and eye protection when handling the birds. Carcasses should be double-bagged and placed in municipal trash. Residents who own poultry should change their clothing and shoes after encountering a dead goose to reduce the risk of transmitting the virus to their flocks. State officials are also urging backyard poultry owners to keep their birds in secure enclosures away from wild waterfowl, disinfect equipment, store feed securely and monitor their flocks for signs of illness. Poultry are particularly vulnerable to the virus, with mortality rates potentially exceeding 90%, according to the Department of Wildlife. Wild waterfowl may carry the virus without showing symptoms, although some species of geese, raptors and game birds can experience significant illness and death. Poultry owners who notice unusual illness or unexpected deaths should contact a veterinarian. High mortality rates among domestic poultry should be reported to the Nevada Department of Agriculture at diseasereporting@agri.nv.gov. Although human infections are rare, exposure can occur through contact with infected animals, carcasses or contaminated surfaces without appropriate protective equipment. Symptoms in humans can include fever, cough, sore throat, difficulty breathing, eye irritation, headaches, body aches, congestion, diarrhea and vomiting. Officials advise anyone who develops symptoms after contact with a sick or dead bird to contact a health care provider and disclose the potential exposure. Additional information about bird flu is available through the Nevada Department of Wildlife and Nevada Department of Agriculture. Source: Nevada Department of Wildlife UPDATE: This article was updated to include new information about the UNR geese being infected. 爱荷华州商业鸡群近一年来首次发现禽流感

Bird flu detected in Iowa commercial flock for first time in nearly a year
新闻简报在爱荷华州萨克县,大约 44,400 只火鸡群被发现感染了 高致病性禽流感 (HPAI) H5N1,这是近一年来首次出现此类 高致病性禽流感。爱荷华州农业和土地管理部报告了这一案件,该案件自 2022 年 2 月以来一直在进行中。自 暴发 启动以来,爱荷华州受禽流感影响的鸟类总数比任何其他州都多,2026 年报告了 7 起 暴发 事件,涉及后院、混合物种或猎禽群。美国疾病控制与预防中心继续将公共卫生风险评级为低,因为没有已知的人与人之间的 传播。作为回应,Kim Reynolds 发布了一份灾难公告,以便更容易地部署资源来遏制和监控 暴发,这通常包括派遣受影响的鸡群和处理死鸟。该州敦促生产者和后院鸡群所有者加强生物安全措施,以防止进一步传播。
查看新闻五要素
时间
2026 年,爱荷华州萨克县在商业火鸡群中检测到禽流感 暴发,该州自 2025 年 12 月以来尚未报告此类 暴发。暴发 自 2022 年 2 月以来一直在持续。
地点与对象
暴发 发生在爱荷华州萨克县,影响了大约 44,400 只火鸡的商业火鸡群。农业部和爱荷华州农业和土地管理部参与监测和响应 暴发。
事件
在爱荷华州萨克县的商业火鸡群中检测到 高致病性禽流感 (HPAI),这是近一年来首次发现此类 暴发。爱荷华州农业和土地管理部报告了此案。
规模、影响与风险
自 暴发 启动以来,爱荷华州受禽流感影响的鸟类总数比任何其他州都多。2026 年,该州报告了后院、混合物种或猎鸟群中存在 7 起 暴发 病毒。美国疾病控制与预防中心将公共卫生风险评级为低,因为没有已知的人际传播情况。
应对、状态与不确定性
金·雷诺兹 (Kim Reynolds) 为萨克县发布了灾难公告,以促进部署州和部门资源来遏制和监控 暴发。爱荷华州农业和土地管理部鼓励生产者和后院鸡群所有者加强生物安全措施。暴发 正在按照标准遏制程序进行管理,包括调度和处置受影响的鸡群。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Bird flu detected in Iowa commercial flock for first time in nearly a year

News Brief
In Sac County, Iowa, a commercial turkey flock of approximately 44,400 birds was found to be infected with highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) H5N1, marking the first such outbreak in nearly a year. The Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship reported the case, which has been ongoing since February 2022. Iowa has had more total birds affected by bird flu than any other state since the outbreak began, with seven outbreaks reported in 2026 involving backyard, mixed species, or game bird flocks. The U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention continue to rate the public health risk as low, as there is no known person-to-person transmission. In response, Kim Reynolds issued a disaster proclamation to allow for easier deployment of resources to contain and monitor the outbreak, which typically involves dispatching the affected flock and disposing of dead birds. The state is urging producers and backyard flock owners to enhance biosecurity measures to prevent further spread.
Original source text (English)
Bird flu has been detected in a Sac County, Iowa, commercial turkey flock. (Photo by Stephen Ausmus/USDA) A case of highly pathogenic avian influenza has been detected in a commercial turkey flock of approximately 44,400 birds in Sac County, according to the Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship. While the HPAI H5N1 outbreak has been ongoing since February 2022, Iowa has not reported an outbreak of the bird flu in a commercial flock since December 2025 . The state has reported seven outbreaks of the virus in backyard, mixed species or game bird flocks in 2026. Iowa has had more total birds affected by the bird flu than any other state since the start of the outbreak, according to the U.S. Department of Agriculture's Animal and Plant Health Inspection Service. SUBSCRIBE: GET THE MORNING HEADLINES DELIVERED TO YOUR INBOX. Gov. Kim Reynolds issued a disaster proclamation for Sac County Wednesday after IDALS's announcement. The disaster proclamation allows state and department resources to be more easily deployed to help contain and monitor the outbreak, which typically requires dispatching the affected flock and disposing of the dead birds. IDALS is encouraging producers and backyard flock owners to "continue bolstering" biosecurity measures to protect their flocks. HPAI is a viral disease that can travel in wild birds and infect wild and domestic birds. Instances of the virus typically rise during bird migration seasons. Poultry producers must report possible cases of the bird flu to IDALS and should contact their veterinarians if they suspect birds with the virus. Some clinical signs include a sudden increase in bird deaths, lethargy, decreased egg production, swelling of head, difficulty breathing, thin-shelled or misshapen eggs and diarrhea. Poultry and egg products remain safe to eat and the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, continue to rate the public health risk of bird flu as low as there is no known person-to-person spread. View five news elements
Time
The bird flu outbreak in the commercial turkey flock was detected in Sac County, Iowa, in 2026, with the state having not reported such an outbreak since December 2025. The outbreak has been ongoing since February 2022.
Location and population
The outbreak occurred in Sac County, Iowa, affecting a commercial turkey flock of approximately 44,400 birds. Department of Agriculture and the Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship are involved in monitoring and responding to the outbreak.
Event
Highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) was detected in a commercial turkey flock in Sac County, Iowa, marking the first such outbreak in nearly a year. The Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship reported the case.
Scale, impact and risk
Iowa has had more total birds affected by bird flu than any other state since the start of the outbreak. In 2026, the state reported seven outbreaks of the virus in backyard, mixed species, or game bird flocks. The public health risk is rated as low by the U.S. Centers for Disease Control and Prevention, as there is no known person-to-person spread.
Response, status and uncertainty
Kim Reynolds issued a disaster proclamation for Sac County to facilitate the deployment of state and department resources to contain and monitor the outbreak. The Iowa Department of Agriculture and Land Stewardship is encouraging producers and backyard flock owners to bolster biosecurity measures. The outbreak is being managed with standard containment procedures, including the dispatching and disposal of affected flocks.

Washington State: H5N1 Detections in Cattle Triple (n=27) - 2nd County (Benton) Affected - Avian Flu Diary

Washington State: H5N1 Detections in Cattle Triple (n=27) - 2nd County (Benton) Affected - Avian Flu Diary
新闻简报《禽流感日记》的报告强调了华盛顿州牛中 H5N1 病毒检出率的上升,截至 2026 年 10 月 29 日报告了 27 例病例,使本顿县成为第二个受影响地区。它还引用了 2026 年 10 月 28 日发表的一项预印本研究,该研究表明 15% 的美国成年人在过去六个月内食用了生牛奶,其中幼儿、老年人和免疫功能低下者等高风险群体被列为家庭消费者。华盛顿州金县公共卫生部警告不要食用生奶,因为存在潜在的健康风险,而疾病预防控制中心则建议不要食用受 A(H5N1) 病毒污染的生奶。该研究于 2025 年 2 月进行,属于自我报告且未经证实,研究指出 44% 的原奶消费者实行家庭热饮 治疗。此外,该报告还提到巴西发生了致命的 H1N1v 感染,该感染是在患者死亡一周后发现的,强调了人畜共患流感 传播 的持续风险。研究结果强调,需要对原奶消费和流感预防继续保持警惕并提供公共卫生指导。

时间
该报告发布于2026年10月27日,活动日期为2026年10月29日。该研究于2025年2月进行,结果于2026年10月28日报告。
地点与对象
该报告重点关注美国华盛顿州,并提到了美国普通民众。它还提到本顿县是第二个受到牛 H5N1 检测影响的县。
事件
该报告讨论了华盛顿州牛群中检测到 H5N1 病毒的情况,病例数增加了三倍(n=27)。它还引用了一项关于生奶消费及其与 H5N1 和其他流感风险关联的预印本研究。
规模、影响与风险
在华盛顿州,牛中检测到的 H5N1 病例增加了两倍,达到 27 例。研究表明,15% 的美国成年人在过去六个月内消费了原奶,其中幼儿、老年人和免疫功能低下者等高危人群被列为家庭消费者。CDC 警告不要食用受 A(H5N1) 病毒污染的生奶,因为存在健康风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
华盛顿州金县公共卫生部就食用生奶的危险发出警告。CDC 建议不要食用被 A(H5N1) 病毒污染的生奶。该研究是预印本和自我报告,没有验证加热方法杀死病原体的有效性。报告还提到巴西发生了致命的 H1N1 病毒感染,该感染是在患者死亡一周后发现的。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Washington State: H5N1 Detections in Cattle Triple (n=27) - 2nd County (Benton) Affected - Avian Flu Diary

News Brief
The report from Avian Flu Diary highlights an uptick in H5N1 detections in cattle in Washington State, with 27 cases reported as of October 29, 2026, marking Benton County as the second affected area. It also references a preprint study published on October 28, 2026, which suggests that 15% of US adults consumed raw milk in the preceding six months, with high-risk groups such as young children, elderly individuals, and immunocompromised persons being cited as household consumers. The King County, WA Dept. of Public Health warns against consuming raw milk due to potential health risks, while the CDC advises against raw milk contaminated with A(H5N1) virus. The study, conducted in February 2025, is self-reported and unverified, and it notes that 44% of raw milk consumers practice at-home thermal treatment. Additionally, the report mentions a fatal H1N1v infection in Brazil, identified a week after the patient's death, highlighting the ongoing risks of zoonotic influenza transmission. The findings emphasize the need for continued vigilance and public health guidance regarding raw milk consumption and influenza prevention.
Original source text (English)
Avian Flu Diary Covering Pandemic and Seasonal Flu, Emerging Infectious Diseases, public health, community & Individual preparedness. NOTE: All AFD blogs are written by a human - any mistakes are solely mine. Thursday, October 29, 2026 Preprint: Why Americans Choose Raw Milk: Evidence from a National Quantitative Survey #19,391 With reports of H5N1 infected dairy herds increasing again (see Washington State: H5N1 Detections in Cattle Triple (n=27) - 2nd County (Benton) Affected), we are seeing an uptick in warnings from health departments about the dangers of consuming raw milk. The King County, WA Dept. of Public Health unambiguously warns: Raw (unpasteurized) milk and raw milk products Consuming raw milk and raw milk products can pose health risks. Raw milk and A(H5N1) virus The CDC recommends against consuming raw milk contaminated with live A(H5N1) virus as a way to develop antibodies against A(H5N1) virus to protect against future disease. Consuming raw milk could make you sick. Why is raw milk unsafe? Raw milk is milk from animals, typically cows and goats, which has not been pasteurized. Pasteurization is a process of heating raw milk to kill germs from the animal that are present in the raw milk. Some people believe that raw milk is more nutritious than pasteurized milk; however, the major nutrients in milk are not affected by pasteurization. Raw milk and raw milk products, such as cream, cheese and yogurt can be contaminated with harmful bacteria and cause serious infections. People with weakened immune systems, young children, the elderly, and pregnant women are at increased risk for severe infections from raw milk and milk products. It is especially important that young children, elderly persons, and persons with weakened immune systems consume only pasteurized milk and milk products. Read the label! In Washington state, it is legal to sell raw milk but raw milk products must be clearly labeled. In the fall of 2024, we saw similar warnings after a number of household pets were infected with H5N1, some presumably from exposure to raw milk and/or raw pet food (see LA County Issues Another Raw Milk Warning). Despite ample evidence of its dangers, most states allow its sale from farms or co-ops, and some states - like Washington - allow it to be sold in grocery stores alongside pasteurized milk. State Laws on sale of raw milk - Credit CDC A 2022 study published in the Journal of Food Protection found that: Results show that 4.4% of U.S. adults reported consuming raw milk at least once in the past year, with 1.6% reporting frequent consumption of raw milk (once per month or more often) and 1.0% reporting consumption once per week or more often. Today we have a new preprint - published Oct 28th - which suggests Americans' consumption of raw milk may be even higher than previously reported. It is based on a survey of 1,075 eligible US adults through an online panel in February of 2025, and it reports roughly 15% of US adults consumed raw milk in the preceding six months. While it is possible that pro-raw-milk enthusiasts were more likely to respond to this survey, recruitment was done through Qualtrics Research Services - using demographic quotas - not an openly advertised public poll. I've posted the abstract below, but those wishing a deeper dive will want to follow the link to read the preprint in its entirety. Why Americans Choose Raw Milk: Evidence from a National Quantitative Survey Sophia Ruser, Cristina Rojo, Katherine J Koebel, Calvin Slaughter, Ece Bulut, Renata Ivanek doi: https://doi.org/10.64898/2026.10.26.26366487 This article is a preprint and has not been certified by peer review Preview PDF Abstract Raw milk consumption is a controllable source of foodborne pathogen exposure in the United States (US), yet existing national estimates of raw milk consumption behaviors are outdated and do not capture emerging factors such as consumers' trusted information sources, sociopolitical influences, and at-home risk mitigation behaviors. We conducted a nationally representative cross-sectional survey of n=1078 US adults to estimate the prevalence of raw milk consumption and at-home thermal treatment practices and identify predictors of these behaviors. Multivariable logistic regression models were used to evaluate predictors of raw milk consumption and thermal treatment behavior using adjusted odds ratios (aORs). Among 1,075 eligible respondents, 15% consumed raw milk in the last six months, including 5% exclusively and 10% in addition to pasteurized milk. In multivariable analysis, raw milk consumption was independently associated with younger age, cohabitation with high-risk individuals for foodborne infections (including children less than or equal to five years of age, and elderly, pregnant or immunocompromised individuals) who had consumed raw milk, and perceptions of raw milk taste and safety. Adults aged greater than or equal to 55 years had lower odds of consumption than those aged 18-24 years (aOR, 0.05; 95% CI, 0.02-0.15), while those living with a high-risk household member who consumed raw milk had higher odds of consumption (aOR, 4.39; 95% CI, 2.69-7.22). Perceiving raw milk to have higher risk of causing illness was associated with lower odds of raw milk consumption (aOR: 0.32, 95% CI: 0.17-0.62) compared to believing the risk to be equal across milk types, and thermal treatment was reported by 38% of exclusive raw milk consumers (n=58) and 47% of consumers of both milk types (n=106). Raw milk consumption in the US appears to be substantially more common than previously reported and shaped by factors such as taste, safety, and household raw milk consumption among high-risk individuals for foodborne infections. The proportion of raw milk consumers already practicing at-home thermal treatment suggests a potential opportunity for risk mitigation. While all of this is self-reported and unverified information, it appears that among the respondents to this poll that high-risk groups (young children, elderly persons, and persons with weakened immune systems) are often cited as household consumers of raw milk. 10.0% reported a child aged five or younger who had consumed it. 9.1% reported an adult aged 65 or older. 3.2% reported an immunocompromised person. 2.1% reported a pregnant person. One potential bright spot, however, is the reported practice of heating or steaming raw milk (44% reported doing so > 50% of the time). Whether their heating temperatures and times were sufficient to kill pathogens is unknown. Since we've seen multiple instances where H5N1 contaminated raw milk has reached store shelves (see California: CDFA Announces Another Recall of H5 Contaminated Raw Milk), the risks - while low - are genuine. But whether any of this will dissuade those currently inclined to consume raw milk - after decades of near universal public health advice to the contrary - remains to be seen. Wednesday, October 28, 2026 WHO: Fatal H1N1v Infection In Brazil #19,390 Overnight the World Health Organization published its latest Influenza at the human-animal interface summary and assessment, 7 October 2026, which describes one previously reported H5N1 case from Cambodia, 4 recent H9N2 cases reported from Mainland China, and one fatal H1N1v case from Brazil. The H1N1v case is unusual in that the adult female patient - who had (unspecified) pre-existing chronic conditions - died after a month-long hospitalization. Of note: Her husband and three adult children also reported flu-like symptoms - were treated for a `viral infection' - but were never tested. According to the timeline provided by this report, the hospitalized case was only identified as having a novel flu (H1N1v) a week after her death. As a result, epidemiological investigation wasn't begun until September 5th, roughly 7 weeks after she (and her family) first fell ill. First, the WHO report, after which I'll return with more. Swine influenza viruses in humans Influenza A(H1N1)v, Brazil On 30 September 2026, Brazil notified PAHO/WHO of a laboratory-confirmed human infection with an influenza A(H1N1)v virus in an adult woman, with pre-existing chronic conditions, residing in the state of Santa Catarina. She developed symptoms of influenza-like illness on 16 July 2026 and sought medical attention on 20 July. Her husband and three adult children, who live in the same household,also experienced symptoms of an influenza-like illness; at that time, all were diagnosed, based solely on clinical examination, with a viral infection and received treatment. The woman, however, did not recover. On 27 July, the patient was hospitalized due to worsening respiratory symptoms. She received treatment, including the antiviral oseltamivir, and required intensive care, including ventilatory support. Despite intensive care, the patient passed away on 28 August. A nasopharyngeal sample, collected on 29 July, tested positive for influenza A on 30 July and was subsequently subtyped as influenza A(H1N1) on 31 July. The sample was sent to the reference laboratory for respiratory viruses at the Oswaldo Cruz Institute (IOC/Fiocruz) in Rio de Janeiro for further characterization. On 4 September, genomic analysis identified an influenza A(H1N1)v virus. According to information gathered during an epidemiological investigation conducted by municipal and state teams between 5 and 9 September, the patient had no history of travel or direct contact with swine. However, a possible indirect exposure to swine was identified through a neighbour who raises these animals for personal consumption. Since 2015, twelve cases of human infection with swine-origin virus variants, including this one, have been detected within Brazil’s respiratory virus surveillance workflow. This chronology illustrates just how difficult it can be to detect a novel virus, even when the patient is hospitalized with severe influenza. A few recent examples include: In November of 2025, Washington State reported the first known human infection with avian H5N5; a diagnosis that was delayed - according to an MMWR report - by `early and repeated negative influenza test results from upper respiratory swab specimens'. In 2024, a Missouri patient was hospitalized with influenza A, and released, before being notified they had H5N1 (see Missouri DHSS: Human H5 bird flu case confirmed in Missouri). Also in 2024, Australia treated a child with recent travel from India for severe flu, which resulted in respiratory failure. The child fell ill (in India) in late February, was hospitalized in Melbourne in early March, and was released after > 2 weeks. While respiratory samples were taken in early March, they weren't identified as H5N1 positive until mid-May. Complicating matters, most people experiencing mild or moderate flu symptoms are unlikely to seek medical care, and even when they do, they are often treated for a `viral illness' and never tested. We've seen past studies (see here, here, and here) suggesting that as few as 1 in 100 cases are identified in the community. In early 2025, the CDC issued a HAN: Accelerated Subtyping of Influenza A in Hospitalized Patients, requesting that all hospitalized patients with suspected influenza be subtyped within 24 hours. More recently, we saw Washington State: Updated guidance for novel influenza screening and influenza subtyping, which emphasized the importance of identifying those with acute respiratory illness (ARI) and specific epidemiologic risk factors. The reality is, it takes a certain degree of luck to detect sporadic novel flu infections in the community. While better screening methods could improve detection rates, many mild and moderate infections will still likely fly beneath our radar. A reminder than anything we say about the current threat from H5N1, swine flu variants, MERS-CoV, or any other pandemic threat must carry an implied asterisk. A disclaimer that says, ` * based on available, and likely incomplete, information. . . '. Tuesday, October 27, 2026 Washington State: H5N1 Detections in Cattle Triple (n=27) - 2nd County (Benton) Affected #18,389 While it hasn't shown up yet on the USDA's H5N1 in Livestock dashboard, the number of dairy herds in Washington State infected with HPAI has jumped from 9 late last week, to 27, with a second county (Benton) now reporting an infected herd. Less than 3 weeks ago (Oct 8th) the USDA/APHIS announced the first detection of H5N1 in dairy cattle in Washington State, which was followed a week later by the first (reportedly mild) human H5N1 infection reported in the U.S. in 2026 (see Washington State Confirms Human Avian Flu Infection). Other than the (above) update to the Washington State Dept. Agriculture dashboard, I have found no other public announcement of this jump in detections. Yesterday, the WSDA did publish a statement on the detection of H5N1 in a second county, which also states: Media note: WSDA will issue a news release for each new county with confirmed HPAI in cattle. Subsequent detections in previously announced counties will be posted online. Excerpts from yesterday's announcement follow: WSDA confirms HPAI in dairy herd in Benton County OLYMPIA – Oct. 26, 2026 – Washington State Department of Agriculture (WSDA) has confirmed a detection of highly pathogenic avian influenza (HPAI) H5N1 in a Benton County dairy herd. The virus was detected by the Washington Animal Disease Diagnostic Laboratory (WADDL) through routine surveillance, and the finding was confirmed by the USDA National Veterinary Services Laboratories (NVSL). The dairy herd is under quarantine and has employed strict biosecurity measures. Veterinarians are working to treat sick cows. Pasteurized milk products remain safe for consumers, and milk supply shortages are not expected. WSDA urges dairy farmers statewide to report any clinical signs of illness to their veterinarian, including: Changes in milk appearance (thicker, often yellowish to brown) Decreased milk production Decline in feed intake Fever Respiratory symptoms Changes in manure Steep drop in rumination and rumen motility If you notice any of these signs, isolate affected animals and contact your veterinarian and WSDA at 360-902-1878 or ahealth@agr.wa.gov. Most cattle affected by HPAI recover with supportive care with full herd recovery typically taking 4-6 weeks. “Seeing the virus move to another county is disappointing but our dairy farmers are working hard to implement their biosecurity plans, share lessons learned and help each other get through this,” said Washington State Veterinarian Amber Itle, VMD, MS. "This isn’t new for the country, but it is new for Washington. I’ve been in awe of how hard our veterinarians and dairy farmers work together every day to keep their cows healthy, keep their workers safe and keep operating despite all the uncertainty this virus brings with it. This detection was through routine surveillance enabling us to detect the virus before cows got sick. We hope this will help us to implement measures on farm to prevent transmission within or between other dairy or poultry facilities.” HPAI can spread between dairy cattle through infected animals and contaminated milk, equipment, vehicles, clothing, footwear and other materials or people that move between farms. Wild birds can also carry HPAI, but they are not considered the primary source of spread of the virus that affects dairy herds. Poultry are also highly susceptible to HPAI. Other states have reported infections in commercial and backyard poultry flocks near dairy farms affected by H5N1. Poultry owners, including those who keep backyard flocks, should remain vigilant for signs of illness and practice strong biosecurity to help reduce the risk of infection. (Continue . . .) According the WSDA, there are nearly 400 dairy farms in Washington State, which suggests that over the past 3 weeks roughly 7% of their dairy herds have tested positive for H5N1. Dairy product safety Pasteurization inactivates viruses and bacteria in milk, including HPAI virus, helping ensure the safety of the dairy supply. Infected cows can shed the virus in their milk, making raw milk and other raw dairy products unsafe for consumption. Avoid raw dairy product consumption, and do not allow your pets to consume raw milk or other dairy products, or raw foods such as raw meat. While the State of Washington posted the above warning on October 8th, Washington allows the sale of raw milk in grocery stores, natural food markets, food co-ops, and direct-to-consumer farm stores. Hopefully, the public is taking these risks seriously. ECDC & CDC Updates On Ebola Multi-country outbreak 2026 Credit CDC #19,388 With the number of confirmed cases nearing 10,000, the Ebola Bundibugyo outbreak in central Africa - which was first announced in mid-May in the DRC - is now the second largest in history, and shows no sign of stopping. Credit CDC This outbreak has spilled over into neighboring countries, and an imported case was recently reported in Nigeria (see ECDC Threat assessment brief – Importation of Ebola disease cases caused by Bundibugyo virus to Kenya and Nigeria). While sporadic exported cases are always possible, the public health risks outside of the DRC and surrounding areas is considered low. B routine priorityPublic-health news

Chicken tenders: bird flu vaccines under attack - American Council on Science and Health

News Brief
The article titled 'Chicken tenders: bird flu vaccines under attack' by the American Council on Science and Health, published on October 29, 2026, raises concerns about the effectiveness of bird flu vaccines. However, it does not report any confirmed or suspected outbreaks of avian influenza A viruses. The piece focuses on the debate surrounding vaccine efficacy without providing specific case counts, death counts, or exposure numbers. It also does not mention any explicit risk levels or health-system impacts. The article does not detail any investigation, testing, isolation, contact tracing, prevention, treatment, or official communication measures related to avian influenza A viruses. The content is a general discussion and does not provide evidence of an active outbreak or specific public health response.

巴克斯特县后院鸡群报告禽流感病例 - 阿肯色州民主党公报

Bird flu case reported in Baxter County backyard flock - The Arkansas Democrat-Gazette
新闻简报On October 28, 2026, the Arkansas Department of Agriculture reported the first case of highly pathogenic avian influenza in the state this season, found in a backyard domestic poultry flock in eastern Baxter County. The virus, which is highly contagious and affects both domestic and wild birds, has caused sudden deaths or severe illness in poultry and poses a risk to the state's $6.7 billion annual poultry industry. The department emphasized the importance of prevention and monitoring, stating that any suspected or confirmed case must be reported immediately. They also have the authority to quarantine infected premises, restrict poultry movement, and require depopulation and disinfection to prevent further spread. Poultry owners are advised to follow safety practices such as washing hands and shoes, limiting exposure to wild birds, and quarantining new birds for at least two weeks.

规模、影响与风险
This is the first case of highly pathogenic avian influenza in Arkansas this season. The virus has caused sudden death or severe illness in poultry and can lead to economic losses in the state's poultry industry, which is valued at more than $6.7 billion annually.

Bird flu case reported in Baxter County backyard flock - The Arkansas Democrat-Gazette

(Special to The Commercial/University of Arkansas System Division of Agriculture) Let us read it for you. Listen now. Your browser does not support the audio element. Arkansas' first case of highly pathogenic avian influenza this season was reported Tuesday by the state Department of Agriculture in a backyard domestic poultry flock in eastern Baxter County. "While this is currently the only detection in Arkansas, increasing detections in other states reinforce the importance of continued prevention and monitoring," the department said in a news release. The avian influenza, or bird flu, is a highly contagious virus that affects domestic and wild birds. It has caused sudden death or severe illness in poultry and can lead to economic losses in the state's poultry industry. Poultry production in Arkansas is valued at more than $6.7 billion annually, the department said. The virus spreads through contact with infected birds, contaminated equipment, or materials. "Under Arkansas state rule, any suspected or confirmed case of avian influenza must be reported immediately to the department," the news release said. "State regulations authorize the department to quarantine infected premises, restrict poultry movement within designated control zones, and require depopulation and disinfection when necessary to prevent further spread." Poultry owners should follow safety practices that include washing hands and shoes before and after contact with birds, limiting exposure to wild birds, restricting visitors to poultry areas, and quarantining new birds for at least two weeks. Poultry owners can also report any unusual illness or sudden deaths in their flocks to the Arkansas Department of Agriculture at (501) 823-1746. × You're one step away from the full story. B 常规重要公共卫生新闻

禽流感 威胁全球秃鹫种群 |新闻明智 - 新闻明智

Avian Influenza Threatens Vulture Populations Worldwide | Newswise - Newswise
新闻简报巴斯克地区 EHU 大学的乔恩·莫兰特 (Jon Morant) 与 IMEDEA 和 INIBIOMA 的研究人员领导的一项研究强调了 禽流感(特别是 H5N1)对全世界秃鹫种群的日益严重的威胁。该病毒已在秃鹫物种中造成广泛的死亡率,特别是在美国和南美洲,数千只美洲黑秃鹫和土耳其秃鹫死亡。中毒等人类活动以及病毒在家畜和野生动物中的高死亡率加剧了秃鹫数量的下降。秃鹫作为食腐动物,在防止病原体传播方面发挥着至关重要的作用,但它们对 H5N1 病毒的易感性造成了一个悖论:随着越来越多的秃鹫死亡,清除受感染尸体的能力就会减弱,从而增加了病原体传播的风险。研究人员提出了五项措施来减轻威胁,包括种群监测、疫苗接种 活动、管理未受污染尸体的饲养站、严格的生物安全协议和针对具体情况的行动计划。这些行动旨在跨境协调并纳入共享治理结构,以保护秃鹫并维持生态系统平衡。
查看新闻五要素
时间
他们还强调了秃鹫数量减少可能对生态系统和人类福祉产生的潜在影响。
地点与对象
全球范围内,特别提到美国、南美、欧洲和美洲。
事件
巴斯克地区 EHU-大学、IMEDEA 和 INIBIOMA 的研究人员进行的一项研究强调了 禽流感 (H5N1) 对秃鹫种群的威胁,指出该病毒已在重要的秃鹫栖息地广泛传播,并造成了严重后果,包括美国和南美洲数千只秃鹫死亡。秃鹫数量的下降归因于禽流感以及其他与人类有关的威胁。
规模、影响与风险
由于禽流感和其他威胁,23 种秃鹫中至少有 16 种受到保护。H5N1 病毒在家畜和野生动物中都有很高的死亡率。该病毒可以通过秃鹫食用的受感染尸体传播,从而导致一个悖论:更多秃鹫死亡会降低消除病原体的能力,从而增加传染风险。
应对、状态与不确定性
研究人员提出了五项行动方针,包括监测秃鹫种群、在可行的情况下实施 疫苗接种 活动、管理未受污染尸体的饲养站、执行生物安全协议以及推广当地行动计划。这些措施旨在减少 H5N1 对秃鹫及其生态系统构成的威胁。
B routine priorityPublic-health news

Avian Influenza Threatens Vulture Populations Worldwide | Newswise - Newswise

News Brief
A study led by Jon Morant from the EHU-University of the Basque Country, along with researchers from IMEDEA and INIBIOMA, highlights the growing threat of avian influenza, specifically H5N1, to vulture populations worldwide. The virus has caused widespread mortality in vulture species, particularly in the United States and South America, where thousands of American black vultures and Turkey vultures have died. The decline in vulture numbers is exacerbated by human activities, such as poisoning, and the virus's high fatality rate in both domestic and wild animals. Vultures, as scavengers, play a crucial role in preventing the spread of pathogens, but their susceptibility to H5N1 creates a paradox: as more vultures die, the capacity to remove infected carcasses diminishes, increasing the risk of pathogen spread. The researchers propose five measures, including population monitoring, vaccination campaigns, managing feeding stations with non-contaminated carcasses, strict biosecurity protocols, and context-specific action plans, to mitigate the threat. These actions are intended to be coordinated across borders and integrated into shared governance structures to protect vultures and maintain ecosystem balance.
Original source text (English)
